"Well, this is an unexpected pleasure," she said. "Who would have thought of seeing you out here." "I had a few I,ouri of: from business to-day,", he said, blushing, "and one seems to get a sniff of the country here." "And the smell cf spring is already in the air," , she replied. "Oh, I am always glad when the spring comes." "You are fortunate in living so near the park," he said. "You think so?"

"There can be no doubt of it. Cheapside makes one hunger for a glimpse of green grass and the sight of a tree, though I believe there is one tree not far away from us. I have not noticed it myself, but at any rate, there is a ttadition to that effect."

She gave a little laugh, then abruptly turned the conversation. "You were going to call on us, of course. We have all been wondering when your promised visit was to be paid. Father would have written you, but you left no address." "I should like to call very much, but I see you are going out." "Only into Marylebone-road. I shall be back again in half-an-hour."

-"May I walk with you?" he said, wondering at his temerity. "If you don't mind," she answered, brightly; "but, as a rule, gentlemen don't like waiting out- side shops." "I don't mind in the least," he said, and they walked away together. They were much more than half-an.hour in going and returning, but to Justin it did not seem five minutes. He wished the streets would lengthen out indefinitely. To walk by the side of this sweet- faced maiden, as though lie were her equal, was like a dream to him, and again came the old ques- tion: "Could he win her? Could he make himself worthy of her? Could he hope that fortune might smile upon him, and work a wonder on his behalf ? " Then his thoughts flew off to Phil. Did he stand in his way? How could he get to know? "Have you seen your cousin lately ? " The question came suddenly and unexpectedly. "Not since I saw him at your house," lie an- swered, colouring slightly.. • "Then you have not told him that you are living in London?"

"No. I have really had no chance." "But why did you evade it when you were at our house? '

"I don't know," he answered, !frankly. "It was his manner, I think. He irritated me some- what."

"I thought you did not seem quite yourself." s For a few moments there was silence, then he said, abruptly: "Have you seen him lately ? " "Oh, yes," and she laughed a merry little laugh. "He drops in once or twice every week—sometimes oftener."

"Lucky dog, Phil I " The next moment she had rung the door-bell. "I found Mr. Pentyre walking unprotected in the park," she said to her mother, with a gay laugh, "so I took compassion on him, and brought him home."

Nothing could have been kinder than Mrs. Love- day's welcome, and Justin found himself once more peeping through the gates of Paradise. An hpur later Mr. Loveday returned. Justin thought he looked'worried and anxious ; and for a while he talked about certain international complications that were upsetting business and causing commercial men no end of anxiety. But over the dinner-table the talk drifted back to Cornwall, and foreign com- plications and business anxieties appeared to be forgotten. Justin not only peeped through the gates, but he seemed to get inside. After dinner Ruby played and sang, and the evening sped away like a dream. And when he left, Mr. Loveday gave him a very pressing invitation to come again. Instead of taking the Underground, he got into a 'bus and dreamt away the journey to Trafalgar- square. Now and then he roused himself and called himself a fool, but he quickly went back to his dreams again. It was getting late when he got to Trafalgar- square, but he decided, nevertheless, that he would walk the rest of the way. The Strand was com- paratively empty, for most of the theatres had closed.

He walked along at a rapid pace, then paused suddenly. "I know that voice," he said to himself. A well- dressed man was reeling on in front of him with a woman holding fast to his arm. He hurried on again, and came close to the couple. The voice was Phil Passmore's—there could be no doubt of that. Thick and guttural it might be, but unmistakably his. • But to make doubly sure, he crossed the street and walked rapidly ahead, then re-crossed it and met the pair where the light of a lamp fell full upon their faces. There was no longer any room for doubt now. Phil was evidently strongly under the influence of drink and every now and then had to be steadied by the painted creature at his side. When he had seen them disappear up a narrow street he walked hurriedly.home like a man hardly awake. Fresh from the simple life of the country, his moral sense was shocked and outraged. In St. lago drunken- ness was looked upon as the parent of all vices, and he had been taught to regard it with utter loathing. And this man was the friend of Ruby, and, for all he knew, her accepted lover. "Oh, I would rather see her buried " he said. "It is horrible—horrible. It cannot be—it must not bet"

Then he paused and looked up at the stars. "Alas!" he said, in a tone of despair, "I am powerless to prevent it! If it is to be, it will be

LADIES' LETTER.

My DE AU IN ELLIE,

I think, as do a great many more of my friends, that Court mourning for a whole year is a very serious matter for British trade. That coloured dress should be forbidden for so long a period to the whole of our aristocracy involves a severe blow to tradesmen who supply textile fabrics and millinery, and also to all manufacturers of materials .other than black in tint. The period decreed is not a day too long for the feelings of the beloved Queen's sorrowing subjects, but our England's interests are inextricably interwoven with trade, for are we not truly, as Napoleon the First described us' a nation of shopkeepers ? The pGriod of general mourning is left to individual inclination, taste or discretion, and it is probable that three weeks will, for the most part, be the shortest time for which it will be worn. "Deepest mourning" are the words of the Order of his Majesty and the Council. This probably means crape, and it will also be interpreted by many.

Now that people have realise . that the national mourning will last for a considerable time, they have turned their attention to seeing which of their clothes can be turned to account in the present emergency, it is better to spend a little on doing up and altering gowns and hats to suit the case than to put these by, for fashions alter quickly, aid by the time we emerge into colours again the relentless tide will have swept on and left the erst- while fashioi tble garment stranded amongst the modes that have been. Black dresses have been the height of the fashion all through the winter, and in the majority of cases only need the removal of a little colour or trimming to adapt them to the needs of the moment. Black hats, too, have been much worn, and most of them hold good now, though all paste ornaments stmuld be removed, and jet or enamel substituted.

are growin,;

, larger and more important; some are almost turbana, in fact. I am sorry to see that the aigrette is a prominent 'feature in all—one, in deep rose panne, had a broad bandeau passing round the front of the head, with a bushy white diarnent6 aigrette at one side and a large bow of the panne, with upstanding ends at the other end of the connecting band. Another was of cream panne, with white aigrette and a very imposing spray of skeletonised silver leaves, the veinings all picked out with diamonds. Bnt perhaps the prettiest was a broad band of twisted seed pearls passing right mind the head, with a large knot of the pearls in the centre of the front, from which uprose a white aigrette.

To make a ___-.Jhie __ get 14 inches of three-and-a-half-inch-wide ribbon divided into two parts. One yard and a-half of two-and-a-quarter- sneh-wide ribbon is gathered in the centre on both sides and sewn to the four inches of the wide ribbon, the remaining three inches of each piece being fringed and lapped over on the outside. The ends of the ribbon are crossed five inches above the pocket and tied in a bow. A monogram or initial may be embroidered on one dap. Pin to dress with a safety p;n on wrong side of pocket. Striped materials are well worn just now, especially in black and white. These stripes, in varying widths, but usually about three-quarters of an inch broad, are very cunningly made up by clever dressmakers, the skirt being cut round the waist, so that the black stripes merge into each other, the white coming into evidence a little lower down, which gives somewhat the appearance of a sunray-pleated skirt, and has a very diminish- ing effect on the hips, beneficial to stout people. rhese black told white dresses look at their best made up with deep capes or yokes and rover and gauntlet cuffs of deep ecru-coloured coarse lace, and many are lavishly trimmed with sable tails. The rather trying lines of a dress may be broken in the front of the bodice by a simulated bolero introduced in the trimming, together with a simulated vest consisting of closely placed rows of black mohair braid. Small buttons and loops fasten the vest up the front. The sleeves are finished with braid and festoons like the bolero, and the undersleeves mai ch the vest. They are gathered into plain wristbands with frills falling over the hands. The skirt of the Princess dress forms a long polonaise, finished with deep van- dykes round the edge and buttoning in scallops down the front. The underskirt is silk with triple rows of braid, set horizontally, as trimming. Some of the silk and wool mixtures, such as Tagus°, Clairette, and drap d'Alma, make ideal dresses. Black cloth is as great a favourite as ever, and will be very much used during the next few months for Court mourning. It should be remembered that all garments Should be neatly mended before they are washed, as a rent usaliy grows larger during the process of laundrying. Stains of all kinds are more easily removed be:ore washing than after. Fruit stains will usually disappear if boiling water is poured through them while they are fresh. If this treat- ment does not prove etnetive, wet the stains several times with a solution of salts of lemon, allowing time for tha goods to dry between the applications. To take out mildew, dissolve a tea- spoonful of chloride of lime in a bowl of hot water, then strain it carefully into half a pail of water. Wet the material in clean water, then put it in the pail and allow it to stand two hours. If at the end of that time the mildew is not remov d, put in more of the solution and try again. Rinse through two waters and hang it up to dry. Unbleached muslin treated in this way becomes beautifully white; a very little chloride of lime bleach 30 or 40 yards.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

According to the Legitimists, Mary Theresa, the wife of Prince Louis of Bavaria, is the lawful Queen of Ecgland. Fr some .1-31"3 past the police have extended their pro,eeien to the wreath a which ,he Jacobites have been accustomed to place en the statue of Charles I, on January 30. But this year they would not allow this display no doubt owing to the notice,which was affixed to St James' Palace on the night of Queen Victoria's death proclaiming Albert Edward to be a usurprr; and that Queen Mary was the lawful Sovereign. In no other country could the spectrea be witnessed of the authoritica openly protecting the symbol of a rival claimant to the throne, but it would seem that we can no longer boast of this, owing to the action of some fool- ish people, who still refuse to recognise the Act of Parlia- ment of 1701. which diverted the throne from the Stuarts to the Hanoverians. Few things could help us more iffectually to realise the death of Qoean Victoria, than the opening of Parlia- ment by the Sovereign in person. Prior to the death of the Prince Consort in 1861, it was the general practice of Her late Majesty to open Parliament, and even after that event, she was present on several occasions at the lbeginnieg of a &Felon. The last time was January 22nd, 1886, on the anniversary of which event she died, fifteen years later. The ceremony was performed in semi state, and the Queen did not wear her crown, which was carried by the Duke of Portland, oar did Her Majesty herself read the speech. Doubtless many of us who witnessed the recent his- torical procession, recalled to mind other processions of a like character, with which bietory has made us familiar. Tha,, for example, on November 11th, 1555, whr n Queen Mary opened her 3rd Parliainent,mest have been one of peculiar splendour. The Queen, magnifi- cently attired, was mounted on strained coarser, whose drcorationa were superb. By her side rode King Philip, wearing his brevet cap.and black velsYt doublet, swords of state being borne before each of them, in token of their independent Sovereignties. And many there must have been among the crowd, the other day, who recalled our own late, beloved Queen's proces.ion, when she opened ber first Parliament, and the thoughts of such must, inevitably have been tinged with sadness. But our confidence in the new King and Queen is firm, and I our affection for them a v,ry real thing indeed, and we are, one and all, full of hope for the future. Pearls and diamonds were the only gems permissible at the opening of Parliament, and many wemen decided therefore to have removed from lovely old heirloom pieces of jewellery, coloured stones, replacing them for the time being with either one or both of the gems approved. Black pearls were few and far between, but hrre they were in evidence, the effect left absolutely nothing to be desired. Few women perhaps care leas for jewels than Queen Alexandra. Her Majesty much Meters flowers, and is never, except when in the very deepest moureing, without a spray, or posy of some description.

QUPPII Alexandra possesses a quantity ef magnificent jewellery, and she now, of course has, for life, the use of some of the Crowe jewels. Soon after Queen Victoria's marriage, the principal Crown jewels passed to the Hanoverian line, and in consequence strained relations between our Sovereign and the Duke of Cumberland always existed. But with Her Majesty's death all is altered. The Duchess of Cumberland, as all the world knows, is a sister of Queen Alexandra, and it is expected that both the Duke and Duchess will now p.y occasional visite to this country. The announcement that presentations at9the court of Queen Victoria, will hold good during the present reign, is a new departure from establiahed cuetom, but it is the simplest way out of a difficulty.. Formerly those members of society who had attended Drawing Rooms had to be presented afresh to the new Sovereign, but since Queen Victoria came to the throne, the number of people who have attained to that privilege has so enormously increased, that it would be almost impossible to individually go through the formality again. Con- seqirently the best way cut of the difficulty was the announcement just issued, which should be satisfactory to all concerned. The bridal gown of Queen Wilhelmina seems to have been as simple as that of the Princess of the Asturias was elaborate. The latter Royal bride,too,wore a very beautiful though somewhat heavy face veil,while the young Queen of Holland was satisfied with one of simple enawy tulle. The effect was much more girlish and delightful in every way. Comparing the trousseaux of the Royal brides, the palm for elaberateness must be given to the Dutch Queen, who, unlike the Spanish Princess, has per- mitted no one bur her mother and her dressers to secure a glimpse of any part of it. Now-a-days the trousseau is a much less extensive affair than formerly, and no more gowns are ordered than will prove sufficient for the honeymoon.. _ _ .

The many society weddings fixed for this month have led to something like a scramble to find suitable days for their celebration before Lent. The wedding day of the Duke and Duchess of Westminster was originally chosen for the 14h, but it was discovered that another wedding, that of Mr. Wilsoe,and Lady Marie Carring- ton, was fixed for the same day, at the same church. The Wiisons cffered to give way to the Dake,and altered their wedding day to the 12tb, but it was after all de- cided that the Westminster marriage could not take place on the 14th, owing to the opening of Parliament by the King, in full state. The duoal wedding was then to have taken place on the 12th,but as the IClsons could hardly be expected to give way a second time, there was no other choice left but fer Saturday the 16 h for the meet interesting wedding of the season. The o'd time superetition about black worn at a marriage ceremony has vanished complettly,and theugh many toilettes in grey, heliotrope, at purple. were to be seen at the recent Society weddings, black was tbe general wear amongst the guests. 'Undoubtedly we are I becoming accustomed to what one might almost say the shock of seeing everyone in sable attire. Our churches, our theatres, all vast garheriege, present one note only— black, and a spot of colour seems as it were an offence. and a blot upon the sombre scene. HARRIS'S., 28-30, BERRY STREET, LIVERPOOL.

_ THE DUKE OF WESTMINSTER.

It is undo-stood that the D uke of Westminster will reside after la's methane at Eaton Ball, the family place near Chrster, during the greater part of every year. Eaton is one of the finest and best-arranged houses in England. It is seven hundred fert in length, and the east^rn front, with its cloisters, is remarkably beautiful, while on this side there is a terrace three hundred and fifty feet long. The main building was only opened for the annual series of house parties during the autumn and winter tu the /ate Duke's rime, as there is a wing for the family which is in itself a compl -t. bonse. The interior is superbly decorated, and there are some good pictures, but tho gems of the Gros, venor collection are kept at Grosnnor FlOtl4f. There are beautiful grounds, which are elaberately laid out, and they include an extensive pinetum. The park is not remarkably picturesque, but it is well tim- bered, and the wooded slopes of the Dee are very pretty. The family wing at Eaton is to be partly redecorated and , refurnished, as the late Duke left most of the best furni- ture in this part of the house to him widow. The old Hall at Eaton was a vast mansion of red brick of no archi- tectural merit, and was pulled down during the reign oi George the Fourth, and replaced with a sumptuous house in the florid ecclesiastical Gothic Style, on which about eight hundred thousand pounds was wasted. This gaudy mansion offended the fastidious ta!te of the late Duke, and he cleared most of it away, and built the present house from d signs by Waterhou.,, of Man- chester, at a cost of nearly a million—World.

CHAPTER XIII.

Telephoge: No. 570.

Ito. 1

Telegraphic Ad'

AR 'UNEXPECTED MEETING. "Alas for him who flings away The shield of purity.' At the mention of Phil's name Justin grew cold all over. He was the one man at the moment he did not want to see. His presence would be like a false note in music. The sweet harmony of the alternoon would be at an end.

• ?bil came bland and smiling into the room, but on catching sight of Justin his face suddenly clouded and his eyes looked unutterable things. It was but for a moment, however. The cloud passed as suddenly as it came, though Justin felt that his greeting was far less cordial than it was wont to be.

Ruby received her guest with a certain shyness and reserve that Justin was quick to notice, but did not know how to interpret. Was it the natural shyness of a maiden welcoming her lover, or But he had no time to debate the question then. Phil was not shy, in fact he appeared to be quite at home. Too much at home for Justin's peace of mind. He received his cup of tea as a matter of course, and then turned his attention to Justin..

"You did not let me know you were coming up ? " he said, in a questioning tone. 'No, I had no opportunity.' "Of course, you are not staying long?' There was something in the manner in which this was said that struck Justin unpleasantly and sent him back into his shell. _ _

"A few days longer," ha answered, evasively and 3You should have let me know you were com- ing," Phil said, suavely, "and I would have made arrangements for shewing you the sights of London."

"Unfortunately, I have very little time for sight- seeing," ups the answer. "Besides, I did not wish to inake fayself a burden to anybody." "Tut! tut I You should not talk about being a burden. Am I not your first cousin?" "But that gives me no right to encroach upon your time, though I should like to call upon you if you have an evening to spare." "Ali, just so. A very good idea. Let me think. Unfortunately, every evening next week is taken up. You should have let me know beforehand that you were coming up, then we could have arranged it. But I'll see about it. Shall I trouble you for another cup of tea?" turning to Ruby. "Thank you very much. I don't know when I have tasted anything so refreshing." Five minutes later Justin felt, as on a former occasion, that he was out in the cold. Phil monopolised the conversation and Ruby also. The talk drifted away to subjects that he knew nothing about. Ruby appeared to become so interested that she did not notice that Justin sat dumb. Phil kept the ball rolling with amazing dexterity, now and then addressing himself to Justin but giving him rib chance of reply. The position became intolerable at length, and he rose abruptly from his seat. "You are surely not going yet?" Ruby said, rising also. "Father and mother will be back—* Then the louder voice of Phil chimed in: "Sorry you have to go, Justin, but I know what it means when a fellow is up on business—" "Bat I took it for granted that you would stay to dinner," Ruby interposed. "You surely—" "You'll remember me to all at home, Justin, if I should not see you again," broke in the louder voice of Phil. "I'm awfully sorry you've dropped on me at such a busy time." And somehow the louder voice prevailed, as it often does in this world. Those who can shout the loudest often get most attention. While Ruby was thinking what she should say next, Phil went and opened the door. It was a hurried and clumsy leave-taking. Justin managed to say to Ruby that he would call again, and then, without quite knowing how, he found himself descending the stairs, and a minute later the street door closed behind him. For some distance he walked on, not knowing where he was or caring. The twin demons of jealousy send despair seemed to be tearing him to pieces. "Oh, I've no chance," he muttered to himself, bitterly. "She's kind and gracious, and all that; but that's her nature. Oh, I wish I'd never :en her, then I might have been content to jog along in the old ruts to the end of the journey. Now I'm trying to catch a comet," and he clenched his bands savagely. A few minutes later he contradicted himself flatly. "I'm glad I've seen her," he said, raising his head and smiling. "And isn't she lovely. Life is worth the living if only for the chance of looking at her now and then and seeing her smile." So he alternated between hope and despair, between Paradise and Purgatory. There were times when he believed that she cared • for him as much as she cared for Phil. Perhaps more, for had he not saved her life ? And the would not be likely to forget that. At other times he was fully per- suaded that Phil was her accepted lover, and that he had no 4ight to bestow another thought upon her. On that point, however, he resolved to satisfy himself at the earliest opportunity. By some means or other he must find out if any under- standing existed between them. During that night he scarcely slept at all. Try as he would he could not help thinking that he had come to London on a fool's errand. His conduct always looked worse in the darkness than it did in the daylight. Supposing Phil had not to be reckoned with. Supposing Ruby, instead of favouring him, hated him, what then? Even if Phil were out of the way his chance would scarcely be improved. He had seen her in her home, and how could he ever hope to make a home for her like that ? He was earning barely enough to maintain himself, and he saw no chance of improving his position for many years to come, if ever. Mean- while, the very idea of a shop assistant aspiring to the hand of a merchant's daughter seemed too ridiculous to be regarded seriously. Nevertheless—such inconsistent creatures are we all—next morning being Sunday and fine, he started off again for Regent's Park. It was not difficult to keep an eye on the Lovedays' house without being noticed. He wanted to find out what church they attended, and whether Phil attended the same place of worship, and whether he joined the family circle after service and went home with them to lunch. A few minutes before eleven he saw Mr. and Mrs. Loveday and Ruby descend the steps and walk slowly up the street. To follow them without being noticed was not difficult. In a few minutes they had entered a church and were lost to sight. "That is a point gamed," he reflected. "But if lam seen here by Phil lie will accuse me of all sorts of things," and he walked rapidly away till he came to another church which he entered. The service proved to be short, and he was back again in time to see the Lovedays come out. He gave a little sigh of relief when he discovered that Phil was not with them, and then hurried off to Portland-road Station and got home in time for dinner. He did not go to Regent's Park again for two or three weeks. He felt a little bit ashamed of him- self, yet all the while lie was pining for a sight of Ruby's face. Towards the middle of March he got half-day off, and he was not two seconds in making up his mind what to do with it. There was already a touch of spring in the air as he walked across the park, and a grateful- earthy smell from the newly-turned flower beds. It made him think of Endilloe, and stirred his heart with a only forful a longing to otselemtwheeveorld pnlaocewaagsaigno.i nglttowsaees n Ruby, and that thought banished everything else from his mind. "Being Wednesday," he reflected, "I am not likely to be thwarted by Phil Passmore unless he calls every day in the week, which is not at all probable." He slackened his pace when the terrace came into sight through the leafless trees. He was wondering if the-Lovedays would be at home, and whether they would think he was presuming upon a chance acquaintance in calling a second Um& "I know I am not of their set," lie reflected, "but Ruby asked me to call, and I promised her I would, but I shall soon discover if lam not wanted," and he walked on still more slowly with his eyes bent upon the ground. Suddenly he looked up with a start and an exclamation of surprise. Standing before him was her lips parted in one of her most gracious

CHAPTER XIV. NOTICE TO QUIT.

" The cloud of my perplexity Grows denser every day.",

When Justin awoke next morning, he was half disposed to think that the experience of the night before was an unpleasant dream. He knew that Phil made no profession of religion, but that he was addicted to drink and to the vices that too often follow such indulgence was almost too painful to be believed.

"At any rate," he reflected, "I will keep the matter to myself until I have had further evidence. "It may have been a sudden fall, and may never occur again." Meanwhile, Justin found it almost impossible to put the matter entirely out of his thoughts. It made him suspicious, watchful, and uneasy. He had no idea of spying on his cousin; and yet, unconsciously having got on a certain trail, he followed it up. This was much easier than would appear at first sight. London is not so big, after all, and men move in comparatively small circles. Within a month of that accidental meeting in the Strand, it was a matter of absolute certainty to Justin that Phil was a sharper and a rake. Even the firm of Mead, Runcorn, and Jago, brokers and commission agents, was only a blind, and, in some instances, a decoy. Mr. Phil Passmore in Regent's Park, was Mr. Jago to such as frequented No. 92, Melton-Friars. The revelation to Justin was altogether sickening; yet what could he do? Phil was his cousin. Should he go and openly denounce him? To whom? Who cared? If Phil Passmore, or a thousand Phil Passmores, chose to waste their substance in riotous living, who was to hinder them? Did not one big section of London live on the follies of the rakes and fools who were in it.

Suppose he denounced him—what then? Phil was too much a man about town not to have an answer. To play shuttle-cock with a man's reputa- tion was a very risky business, and might land him in gaol before he knew what had happened.. But the Lovedays ought to know. No doubt they ought. Perhaps they did know. But fashionable society did not trouble itself much about such things. Men often got light-headed and hilarious over dinner, and no one thought the worse of them. But—but— Oh, well, such "huts" were not considered. Young men were expected to sow their wild oats, and no one was supposed to ask any questions. Justin was not slow-witted, and it was impossible to live in a big business house in the heart of the City without learning a hundred things that were a sealed book to him before.

In IreHord everybody knew everybody else. There were very few secrets in it. Nobody sould take an office without everybody being acquainted with the fact, and without everybody knowing also the character of the business he did. In fact, people haa so much time at their disposal that they could not only attend to their own affairs but to the affairs of everybody else. That was one of the charms of living in a place like Treford. Nobody could feel alone there or forsaken. Every- body took a kind of paternal interest in all the rest. If you bought a new umbrella, it was known, and also how much you paid for it. If you sneezed twice, it was matter for common remark before evening. But in Cheapside Justin quickly realised the eontrast.

Once he asked the riiiest ion : "Who occupies the offices on the next floor?" and was politely told that he had better and ironire. The City man does not know who 11;s 1:::-hour is, and what is more, he does not care. Irc is so busy with hit own concerns that he has no time to think about other people's. Justin worried himself a great deal about Phil but he appeared to be the only one who did so. And yet, if the truth must be told, it was not Phil's moral peril that was the chief cause of hie anxiety—it was the thought of this unredeemed rout becoming the husband of Ruby Loveday. That prospect was bitter enough to contemplate when he believed that Phil was worthy of her, but now that he knew he was absolutely unworthy, it wag positive torture.

He did not call on the Lovedays again for some weeks. He was terribly afraid of overreaching hie welcome. He did, however, go to the church they attended every Sunday morning. Not that he vette impressed by the service or the preacher. In fact, he liked neither; but he caught a glimpse of Ruby, and that was more to him just then than any sermon preached in London.

On the first Sunday he went very early, and in- quired of the 'sexton where the Lovedays sat, then he took a seat behind a pillar where he could see with very little chance of being seen. Ruby, in her devout Sunday attitude, seemed more bewitching than ever. To her the service was evidently no meaningless form; she entered into the spirit of it, and found in it real joy and inspiration. And as Justin sat and watched her pure, thoughtful face, and then contemplated the possibility of her marrying Phil Passmore, he grew sick at heart. But what could he do? The old question was constantly cropping up. He could not tell Rube, He could not soil her ears with such a narratilks: And if he told Mr. Loveday would he be believed r Phil was evidently a friend of the family, and his word would be taken first; and if he denied it, as he doubtless would, then he would be discredited, and perhaps forbidden the house. Moreover, it is not always , easy to prove what you know to be facts, and to speak evil of one's own relatives is a sorry business at the best. "I can do nothing, he would say to himself, bitterly. "If I were to breathe a word' he would have me up for slander. My motives would be impugned, and I should be regarded as a sneak and a traitor." So the days andiCeks passed slowly away and he found what comfort he could in watching Ruby from behind the pillar. Those were the only moments of sunshine in his dull, colourleselife. In the City every day seemed like every other. The same weary routine had to be gone through, and he saw no hope of change or improvement. He had come to London to make his fortune, and he was no nearer the realisation of his hopes than at the start. Talk about there being no scope in Trelford. What scope was there shut up as an assistant in one department of a big City house. He might remain till he was grey, and blind, and bald, and still be serving behind the same counter. Often enough in his duller moments lie called himself a fool for ever leaving home, and a bigger fool for giving a second thought to Ruby Loveday. "Why should I worry myself ? " he would say to himself, angrily. "She can never be anything to me: and whether she marries Phil or somebody else, what can it matter? If she does not get him for a husband she may get somebody a great deal worse. But in any case lam out of it." But the stern deductions of logic are never of any account to the young man who is in love. Ho deliberately closes his eyes to facts and goes steadily on, believing all things, hoping all things, enduring all things. One Sunday morning in May, the weather being lovely, Justin, instead of returning to the City after church, got lunch at a small restaurant in the neighbourhood, and then sauntered back again into the park. It was very delightful to hear the birds sine and feel the soft, breath of the wind and smell the thousand perfumes that were floating about. As the afternoon wore away all the chairs got occupied, and crowds of people sauntered under the trees and evidently enjoyed the balmy air. Among the rest came Ruby Loveday, on her NvEts, back from a mission-room, where she had been teaching a Sunday. school class. Justin was looking absently across the great stretch of park thinking of home, when Ruby paused in front of him and smiled. Instantly he looked up and their eyes met. If it was spring-time before, it was summer now. Ruby was always gracious with him. "You have come out to enjoy the spring sun- shine?" she questioned. - "I have, Miss Loveday. Besides, all this great stretch of green makes me think of home." "Then you still love the country ? " "Yes, I think I love it more than ever." "But when people want to make their fortunes—" she said, with a mischievous twinkle in her eyes. "Please do not make fun of me," he answered, interrupting her, "for I have discovered that even in London fortune is only for the few." Suddenly her face grew grave. She did not like to ask what he was doing or bow he was succeed- ing, though she was very curious to know. This grave-faced young Cornislimm int-rest-PA •r_more than she knew. She often fompl.l—, culat- ing on his chances of suc.,- her good wishes could have helred .I,ture would have been assured. "You will come home anti havo tea with us?• she said at length. "It is very kind of you—" he Legan. "No, please do not begin to invent an excuse," she interrupted, with a laugh. "I am sure father would be quite disappointed if he knew you were so near and did not call." "It is always a pleasure for me to call," he answered, frankly, only I do not like intruding upon people on a Sunday." "Then it is settled," site said, gaily, and they walked away together. But the walk extended much further than they enticipated. They began to talk about Endillcee and both grew so interested that he was bold enough to suggest that they should take another turn round a portion of the park, and as Ruby raised no objection the walk and the talk lengthened out and out. That was a red-letter day in Justin's life. He often looked back to it in the dull and dismal days that followed and tried to live it over again. As they drew near the house they caught sight of Phil Passmore standing at the drawing-room window watching them. Justin instinctively turned and looked at Ruby, and he saw the warm colour mount quickly to her neck and face. What did it mean? Was Phil her lover? And, if not, why did she blush at sight of him? Phil was all smiles to Ruby, and she blushed again when he came to meet her. But Justin had never known him so frigid. But that might be partly his own fault, for he had grown so to despise his cousin that he could hardly help shewing it. Soon after tea Justin rose to go. He felt less free than he had done before, and somehow con- versation flagged, and no one seemed entirely at his ease. Phil rose at the same time, and, strangely enough, no one asked either of them to stay. They passed out of the door side by side, and walked away down the street together. For awhile they walked on in silence, then Phil cleared his throat and spoke. "You have scarcely been as frank with said, Justin, as I should have expecte ,

"Of course, I don't want to complain," he said, in an off-band manner, "but, seeing I am your cousin, I hardly expected that you would try to hide from me the fact that you had taken a situation here." "I was anxious not to bother anyone with my affairs." "Oh, that is all stuff and nonsense. Friends, and especially relatives, should be perfectly open and frank with each other." "Do you think so ? " "I do. And to prove it I want to tell you a bit of -very interesting news." "Indeed." "Well it is interesting to me. The truth is it is now practically settled that Ruby Loveday an( myself become engaged." "Is that so ? " Justin questioned, with a gasp. "It is, my boy. It will be publicly announced in a very few days. Why don't you congratulate me ? " For a few mrsments Justin breathed hard, then he looked up and said, in a hard, unnatural voice: "You wanted me to be perfectly frank with you?" "Exactly. Why shouldn't you be ? " "Then may I ask first if she knows what von

are?" "What I am ? " "Yes. Have you told her the kind of life you lead. Have you told her that you get drunk, that you are a gambler and a libertine ? " Phil stopped suddenlv short and clenched his

lists. "Good Heavens!" he said, "what do yoti mean P" "I am being perfectly frank with you," was the slow and deliberate answer. "I have seen you drunk in the streets, seen you in company that no pure-minded man could be in. I have discovered something of the kind of life you are living in London, and lam ashamed of you. And for a man like you to even think of marrying a girl like Ruby Loveday is a sin and a crime in the sight of Heaven." "So ho! A new Solomon come to Judgment," and Phil laughed cynically. ' "And you are not ashamed of yourself?" he luestioned, with flashing eyes. "My poor, innocent cousin," and Phil laughed igain. "Well, really, I did not think you were luite so green. Suppose Igo out to dinner and get a little top-heavy, what then? And suppose ifter dinner I get into company that you with your prudish notions don't approve, what then? It's what every young fellow does." "It's a lie!" Justin roared. "There are thousands of young men in London who are as pure-minded as all young men ought to be." "Pah! shop assistants. Molly-coddlers from le country. Y.M.C.A. young men. I am not talking about milk sops, but about men." "Men, indeed!" Justin said, scornfully. "And 3o you mean to tell me that Ruby Loveday knows that you are of this type?" "Of course she does, you innocent," and he laughed again. "Do you think any girl of grit would want a molly-coddle?" "Then God help her," he said, bitterly, and, turning suddenly on his heel, he strode off in the apposite direction. On the following Saturday when he went for his month's pay he received two months' instead, and was informed by his employer that his services I were no longer required. (To be continued.)

LOYAL ORDER OF ANCIENT

SHEPHERDS. Annual Report of the Warrington District. •

The annual report of the Warrington District of the Loyal order of Ancient Shepherds has just been issued. The report is signed by Thomas Miller, Pro. CS.; John Da'ey, D. Pro. C S., James Parr, die. trea,... and Peter Edgar, dis. secry. It is pleasant for Ui to reflect. Oa state that on the While, notwichstanrting, th expend is ture for the year having been again eacep'renall; heavy' in regard to sickness and death Claims. steady, and in many instances satisfactory progress has been made both financially and numerically. During the year 104 members were admitted, while they lost by suspensions 56, and by deaths 17, thereby leaving the presero membership of the district at 943, or a net gain of 31. Financially the combined funda or the district and lodges amounted to £4,838 lie rid as against £4,683 12s St the precfse jog year, or an increase of £152 184 BP. The: large sum of £659 18i 4.d had been paid be the various lodgee to the sick members, being £2B 3s 81 more than the previous year and the total amount paid in discharge of death claims had been £274 as against £192 for me year 1899. After a reference to the death of Grand Treasurer Hawkins and General Secretary Duncan Kennedy, the report proceeds to state that events in these days pass so quickly that while prr paring the report, the people of this vast and mighty empire had bren plunged in sorrow and mourning through the death of Queen Victoria, our well-beloved Sovereigu. Her reign had been a long and glorious one. Great things had happened during the last century, and specially since her accession to the throne. The condition of the people, socially, morally, intellectually and reli2,iously halo wonderfully improved. Progression all along the hne had been by leaps and bounds. It might probably be thought by some that it was not within the province or in the scope of that report to praticularise, but rather perhaps to generalise events. They could not, however' refrain from stating that there was a marked contrast' beta een the privileges that even Friendly Societiee enjoyed to-day. and what they were at the beginning of the century. At the present time not only the membere ,1 of the Royal Family, peers of the realm, judges, mayors. ljustices,and gentlemen of locsl influence and power were identified and associated with them in the various friendly societies, but the late beloved Queen was, the time of her death, a patron of one of tee courts in a kindred society,whereas in the early part of last century' lit was illegal for societies like their own to assemble so, transact the ordinary business of the lodge. They ha great cause to be thankful to the great Shepherd eL them all, as a nation,and as individuals for the happier' state of things that now existed, and for His goodness in giving them such a wise and good Queen.

LICENCE ENDORSED.

• More then usual interest was manifested in the Proceedings at the Warrington Police Court on Mon- daveicensed victuallers and members of the general Public being present in large numbers, The case Which had aroused 60 much interest was one in Which John Taylor, the licencee of the Unicorn Inns Winwick-road, was charged with having Permitted drunkenness to take place on his licensed premises on Saturday. February 2nd. There Was a full bench, presided over by the Mayor (Alderman Roberts). _ -4.--- REMARKABLE PEDESTRIAN FEAT BY A WOMAN.

A remarkable pedestrian feat has recently been per- , formed by a woman living in Crown-street, A eworth, near Ilkeston, Derbyshire. A "Nottingham Exereas " reporter, he.ring of the circumstances, visited her home and interviewed her on the esubject. Her name is Gertrude Morley, and she is. the wife of a miner. She inf irmed the reporter that when 18 years of age she began to be troubled with anremia. This became chronic, and for some time she was under the care of a doctor. After a time she was able to return to her work, but the improvement was of short duration, and soon she was worse than ever. This state of things lasted until she was 22 yeare of age. She had then been married about twelve months, and went to reside at Aws- worth, two miles from Ilkeston. Sleep seemed to afford her no benefit. See always felt tired and I weary, as though she would like to lie down and not get up any more. She bad also a swelling across the stomach, and suffered most severely from constipation. She had lest all pewer to enjoy food, and with the loss of appetite went also the ability to digest fox'. So fear- fully emaciated and exhausted did she become that she could only walk a few steps. Even after so slight an exertion as that entailed, she was entirely " done up," One day when in Ilkeston her huebend heard of Chat. Forde's Bile Beans, and purchased a box Be gave them to his wife, who began to take them regularly. Before the has was finished, she felt a great improvement in her condition. Then came the feat which surprised not only herself and husband, but all her friends who had known lof her condition. She had been one day to her mother's house at Ilkeston, two miles away. Upon returnine, having walked the four miles, she found her husband on the p Ant of setting out for Ilkeston. To his great cur. prise she proffered to accompany him. He at first I thought she must be joking, knowing how ill she had , been only a short time back. She persisted, however, and in the end he yielded. N. one could have been more surpr sed than he wao wheu he found that she had per- formed what for her was certainly a great pedestrian feet of waikmg eight miles without the sliehttst treed of exhaustion. WARRINGTON.

A Child's Sad Death.

_ Aniuquiry was conducted at the Police Station on Monday, by Mr. S Brighouse, County Coroner, into the circumstance attending the death of Edith Ethel [Judson, aged twelve months, who resided with her parents at 16, Gilbert-street —The te- ceased's mother gave evideact3 to the effect that at seven o'clock 02 Tuesday evening, the sth lust, she was preparing her husbond's tea. he brewed the tea ia a jug, which she place i in the oven. Whilst her back was turned the deceased opened the oven door, took hold of the jug, and upset it, the con- tents falling on her neck and right arm. Lime water and oil were at once applied and the osild was taken to the Infirmary. On Thursday she was found to be suffering from scarlet fever, and she was removed to the infectious diseases hospital, where she died the following day—A verdict of " Accidental dah " was returned.

A STRONG MAN BREAKS DOWN.

Mr. William Leonard Ansell is a smith by trade, aged 30, and married, living at 8, Queen-street, Hemel Hempstead, Herts. To a reporter of the Hemel Hempstead Gazette, he made the following statement : In 1899 I was working in the Armstrong big gun factory at Openshaw. Manchester, but after I had been there a few months I felt a loss of nerve power, and was attacked with fainting fits. Time passed and I got no better. More than once I almost fell down at my work. My head became dizzy and my eyesight weak. But worse than that were cold perspirations. I could sit in front of a roaring fire with streams of sweat running down my face and yet feel cold the whole time. At last I had to give up work and come home, but I hadn't been in Hempstead very long before the pains returned more violently than before. So bad did I get that I couldn't sit still long enough to eat my meals. I tried doctor's medicine, and a public institution, hut felt no better. I couldn't understand what was the matter with me, though one doctor described it as nervous debility. "This state of affairs went on for several months, until I thought I had gone past recovery, when one day a friend said, 'Why don't you try Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People? I believe they would do you good. "By this time I was beginning to lose faith in the medicine I had been taking, so I agreed to try a box of the pills and bought some. Before I had taken six pills I felt a change for the better. I coetinued taking them, and before I had finished the box they had purled me round splendidly. Since then I have had more, and am now in as good health as ever I was, which I believe to be due entirely to Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. I can honestly say they did me a world of good." Mrs. Ansell remarked in corroboration: "I shall never forget one Sunday. He turned as white as a corpse, and walked about as though he didn't know what he was doing. He used also to walk the room for hots in the night, being unable to sleep. It was a very anxious time, but now he is quite well again. We shall never be able to say too much for what Dr. Williams' Pink Pills have done for us." Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People have cured innumerable case of various forms of nervous dis- orders, including Paralysis, Locomotor Ataxy, St. Vitus Dance, Rheumatism, Sciatica, Neuralgia, and Early Decay; also all diseases arising from impoverishment of the blood, such as Scrofula, Rickets. THE session which Parliament, Crown, opened on Thursday and People.

marks an important point in our Parliamentary history. It was inaugurated by the Sovereign in person, and the close relations. between Crown and Parliament which had fallen into abeyance will be resumed and strengthened. It may be taken for granted that for many years to come the KING will attend Parliament in per- son, and read his own Speech. He may even be a conspicuous figure in the Prorogation,which is apt to be a poor spectacle. Tee change will be notable in many ways. It will revive the scenic attrac tions of a great national event, and, perhaps, do something to elevate the character of Parliainent itself. The attendance of peers at the opening of the session is sure to be considerably increase& The first business of the present session is to renew and, it may be, to revise the arrangement by which a fixed amount is substituted for the revenues of Crown property and old taxes which are, by a constitutional fiction, claimed and surrendered by the Crown to the Government of the day. If we could put an end to the fiction,so much the better. We no longer recognise the right of:the Crown to obtain income without the ssent of Parliament, and it is now part of the duty of Parliament to raise money for the maintenance of the army and navy, instead of assigning taxes to the Crown for that purpose. When the Civil List has been examined by a special committee it will be the duty of Parliament to make a new understanding, which will continue during the present reign. An allowance for the QUERN CoNsoar is one of the new duties that will be imposed. The late Renews CoNSORT had. £50,000 a year, and probably a like amount will be assigned to the QUEEN CONsoRT. There will be no desire to hamper the 31rown by want of means, and if we are to have spectacular displays we shall have to pay for them. The expenses of the late QUEEN'S funeral and the present KING'S Coronation (which may be an accomplished fact before Parliament:can meet next year) will have to be borne by the State, and the cruise of the Dux eOF CORNWALL to the Colonies, extending to Canada and the Cape, is another matter that will have to be provided for out of national moneys, to be voted, we should magine, in the present session. We offer no objection to these things, and the couetry will gladly provide them, in a spirit of loyal generosity. It is unfortunate for the taxpayers that they should come when other burdens are so heavy and so impossible to evade, but the fact not does effect our duty in the matter. The session must of necessity be largely occupied with financial matters. The war will entail further expenditure as well as further taxation, and all parties must combine to make the latter as equitable as possible. The proportion to be found by the wealthy may very well be increased, though no measure to make such an adjustment can be yet suggested or is likely to find much favour with the classes as distinct from the masses. The local taxation doles come to an end with the present financial year, and the Government will probably ask for their continuance. Owing to several causes, some involuntary in their origin, no revision of the general incidence of local taxation hale taken place. Our statesman have been in no hurry to do the work, and they have had the advantage of the electoral assistance of the classes they ha" relieved at the expense of the rest of the commun- ity. The general financial situation is thus con- siderably mixed, if it be not grave. Whichever way we turn more expenditure has to be faced, and the people will have to contribute more to keep the State machinery going, whether they like it or not. Policy controls expenditure; and as the policy has been fixed by the people, the people must pay.

Wx could hardly expect Mr. Burdett-Coutts' th Criticisms. au mr. ISIIIIDETT-COUTTS., M.P., would be satisfied with the report of the South African Hospitals Commission. It was not intended to please him, or to make good his statements. Ile was badly treated by his own side, and if he had not made such damaging statements, held to be incrimina- ting by independent observers, there would have been no Commission at all. The Commission was selected and appointed to vindicate the Govern- ment, and not to elicit the whole truth. It has I been unable to do either of these things, and hence its satisfactory character. Mr. BuRDErr- A Courrs now contends that "the true state of 11 , things was deliberately hidden from the nation" in the first place, and that there was" a studied campaign of departmental whitewash" in the second. The masterly way in which he handles the results will be held byt utie public to justify his criticisms. The Commission could not compel the attendance of witnesses, and it had no power to protect them, so that some witnesses did net attend. When Mr. BURDETT-COUTTS called the attention of the Commission to the fact that some of the men who had given him information were willing to give evi- dence he was met by the astounding statement that the Commission had decided to hear no more evidence. The witnesses in this country must have been considerable in number. Mr BURDETT- COUTTS puts them at between three and five thou- sand. How many of them were examined? The Commission went to Netley, and it examined seventy men there in a single afternoon ! Mr BIIRDETT-COUTTS makes the singularly pertinent comment: "I cannot find that any patient was examined who was in the famous field hospital at ,

Bloemfontein, to which the report devotes two whole pages, and through which at least a thou- sand men must have passed." If this passage accurately represents the case, the scandal seems to be one of the grossest ever arising out of a national inquiry. The Bloemfontein case was the worst of all, and the field hospital was the worst part of the Bloemfontein case. Hundreds of the men who were in the field hospital succumbed te enteric fever, and could tell no tales. Surely some survivors remained, whose evidence ought to have been material, and why witnesses from other hospitals at Bloemfontein were taken in preference is only intelligible on the theory that whitewashing was designed and Carried out. There were four hundred and sixty- two witnesses examined,but only one hundred and seventy-four of them were patients. Out of fifty- six military officials seven gave evidence against the hospital arrangements. Of the army medical Officers and attendants ten were against the general finding and one hundred and eleven were favourable. The independent witnesses, (lectors and attendants were one hundred and four in number, and fifty-five were adverse to the general arrangements—or, to put it in Mr. BURDETT- COUTTS' own words, "one-half of the independent Witnesses have given evidence directly against the medical arrangements, while only one-tenth of the official witnesses have expressed themselves dis- satisfied." These facts and figures tend to deprive the work and finding of the CJairnission of any substantial and permanent value. If it be replied that the Commission has censured many of the arrangements, despite this limited inquiry,we have only to remark that if a proper inquiry had been made the censure must have been still more severe and the country might then have .been better astisfied. The net result is that the Commission did not do its work properly, and that its guarded knd limited censures and suggestions were mainly irltended to throw doubt On Mr BIIIIDETT-COUTTS' Statements, and to convince the public that enor- Mous efforts had been made, and, on the whole, With satisfactory success. Mr. BIIRDETT-COUTTS Was to be suppressed. Happily,:he is not the man to be smothered by official opinion or a superficial report.

Newton Conservatives are fortunate people. At their annual meeting they are always treated to a speech from the member, Colonel Pilkington, and the ex-member, Lord Newton, and as far as we know the treat is appreciated. The text of the speeches at the last two annual meetings has been the same, i.e., the war, about which both gentle- men have a good deal to say. We have boon struck on both occasions with the contrast in the Speeches and opinions expressed by these two loyal supporters of the Government. Colonel Pilking- ton appears to speak first and think afterwards; at any rate the reverse is certainly not his method or he would not practically pooh-pooh the huge Waste of life and money which is still going on over the miserable South African ,business. "These considerations," says he, ought not to affect to any gteat extent a great country like onrs. . . . . . If this war costa two hundred millions it is not a very great 'flatter." Oh, indeed ! ' the bereaved parent and the apprehensive taxpayer will probably say to ,thern.elves, 'such considerations affect us. Eng- -ISnd is a great country, but she cannot pour out hfe and treasure with such a fine disregard for the Consequences as actuates Colonel Pilkington. He even invites his hearers to remember that a war at beginning of last century lasted :22 years ' srily he is a Job's Comforter.

Lord Newton is much the more thoughtful and Weighty speaker' and while one cannot entirely agree with what he says, there is evidently so much cairn Consideration and earnest conviction behind k "15 OPirlloll3 that they command ourrespect. His Lordshipis somewhat of a pessimist, but we prefer the pessimist to the optimist who talks lightly of lives and millions. He also places a certain amount of blame on the Government for "fatal optimism," and the false assurance some months ago that the War was "at an end." To all that we say "hear hear," but w 3 do not see eye to eye with his Lord- ship in regard to conscription—a modified form of which is a pet theory of his. While it is true that the voluntary system has broken down under . f. 6 recent, strain, it is more than questionable 17nother compulsory military service is tiae remedy, The voluntary system itself weidj not have ,broken down but for the mismanagement and starving of it by the authorities prior to the war. The worth and necessity, of the citizen soldiers has only just be- gun to be fully realised, and already the facilities for affording volunteers competent training are be- ing improved. It is in this direction that the l'eoledy, in our opinion, lies. Let the volunteer and yeomanry forces be properly treated in times of peace and there will be no more need to talk of conscription. &tidied neglect and a deaf ear• to all appeals would cause the breakdown of any sys- tem oi home defence. The fault lies not in the System, but in its adequate application.

The supporters of change-ringing in this country Will he interested to learn that the modern system of fixing the bells in a frame,and working them by a lever wiiich makes the tongues to strike and pro- ' dee • just the proper vibration, is a success in New Yolk. Several churches have adopted it, one of the advantages being that the mechanism can be controlled by one or two men, and the sweating of a large band of ringers, one man for each rope, is "olded. But a great tradition in the art of Fatnpanology is being broken down by the modern invention. We can understand the feeling which rflakes the people of New York cling to the idea that at least there shall be bells, even though they not move. It is possible, of course, to produce Inuch the same result with tubas, and they have °Ken adopted, in some instances, even in ao c.on- 4vative a country as England ; but associations. "tte against them. There can be no poetry in the • 11°11ght of striking tubes. It may be prejudice, ';):11t it suggests rather a hot-air apparatus than Longfellow, Mrs. Haitians, Mrs. Sigourney and Gray could not have got lyrical over a patent el that sort. Macaulay, was right when he fore- st the decline of polpy with the advance of -Knowledge.

It is, perhaps, unnecessary for us to curnmend to the people of the town, the great united mission which commences simultaneously on Sunday in the free churches all over the country. Such a scheme needs no commendation, but we would re- spectfully and earnestly impress the importance, ' even solemnity of it, upon es ery man and woman, ' and every child who is old enough to think. There 'has never been so united and gigantic an effort 11 made for the Christianising of the masses as will be put forth by the united workers of the free churches during the ensuing week. Missions, great and small, there have been in plenty, and each of them has been effectual in furthering the cause of religion some little—but only a little. There are so many obstacles to the spread of Christianity that it does not make any- thing like the progress that is assolutely essential for thiq great nation's cm- tinned prosperity a i supremacy. Are we not told that " righteousness exalteth a nation" ? National righteousriss is therefore the ideal we must look to, both as professed Christians and practical men. Doubtless, seine day, all the churches of the laud will unite in one great mis- sion, such as that inaugurated by the Free Churches. Then, and not till then, can there be any prospect of national righteousness. The time is,perhaps,not quite ripe for so grand a consumma- tion of the Christian's desire,but the Free Churches are undoubtedly furthering it very materially by the united mission. They command a vast organ- isation, And with their intentness of purpose and ' strength of unity, they should accomplish that which will be the equivalent of a long step for- ward in religion. It is a happy augury for tiae twentieth century that this great Christianising effort is being made, and we hope it will be abun- dantly blessed.

A correspondent suggests in another column that Warrington should have a lasting memorial of Queen Victoria in the shape of a new ward at the Infirmary to be called after her, and to be foueded by popular subscription. The idea is an excellent one. Our local Infirmary would be none the worse for an extension, seeing that the demands upon it so rapidly and continuously increase, and further- more, the memorial would be one which our late beloved Queen, were she alive, would have heartily commended. It was one of Queen Victoria's most striking and lovable characteristics that she ever took the deepest interest in the work of our great infirmaries and; hospitals, and frevently visited ! them for the purpose of bestowing on the sufferers her welcome, womanly sympathy. Wesaould like I to see Mr Pendlebury's suggestion taken Up. There is little doubt that the loyal people of Warrington would willingly contribute their shillings on behalf of an object so completely in harmony with the wishes of the sovereign they loved, and in itself so excellently useful.

MATTERS OF MOMENT.

A Provincial Letter.

The country has scarcely had time yet to give I ll expressio i to the satisfaction caused by the King opening Parliament in person on Thursday. Many years have elapsed since the ceremony was previously pe-formed by the Sovereign; the last occasion being in 188 G, when Mr. Gladstone's Home Rule proposal first took definite shape in politics. The ,Session is practically the first of the new Parliament and of the twentieth century, as well as of the new King; and it is generally recognised that his Majesty acted in a praiseworthy and patriotic manner in not allowing private grief to interfere with public duty

The whole country is, moreover, gratified to find that the visit of the Duke of Cornwall to the Aus- tralian Colonies is not to be abandoned, buv, that he will leave England about the end of next month or the beginning of April, on his impo taut mission. The birth of a Colonial commonwealth is, indeed, a great occasion, and one in which the English pr)- vinces have manifested a deep concern. Our late revered Sovereign's resolve to send the then Duke of York to open in her name the first Session of the Australian Federal Parliament was intended to give as it will give, unqualified delight to all who were then her subj erg. It will be one more link in the bond which the stirring events of the past year and m ,re have only forged more closely than over between th 4 British Colonies and the Motherland. It was altogether another of Queen Victoria's happy thoughts. Our cousins at tbe Antipodes could not fail to see in it the" Special proof" of their Sovereign's interest which .her Majesty a Torling to the text of her message, desired to show, and even now it will prove a felicitous acknowledgement of the devotion to the Crown and Empire which the Colonies have so practical y displayed. The Federal Parliament will open its deliberations with a real speech from the throne, and the Duke of Cornwall may be t•-usted to fulfil this agreeable task after the graci- ous manner ,f his late royal grandmother, or of his father the King.

--- It appears that in many places the public have begun to sing the National Anthe nas God save our Lord the King." But surely it were better to alter the "God save our gracious Queen "to " God save our gracims King," as well as being better poetry.

To what base usage may not the universally admired khaki of a few short months since come, if what is now reported of it in the papers be trues! It is there said that a tailor, in a large way of !nisi- ness, is offering his whole stock of Khaki at what he calls a "ruinous price," and in making the an- nouncement the tailor generously offers, in case his customers do not like the Khaki, to sell it as "dyed cloth "Imagine our being ashamed of Khaki, and so soon too!

The rricent alarming and fatal accident at Liver- pool has led, as might be expected to a good deal of wordy warfare in the daily papers. Able and expert writers have proved incontestably that there is much to be said on both sides, as regards the overhead or underground systems of electric trac- tion Those who insist up m local authorities sanc- tioning only the underground arrangement cer- tainly appear to overlook the facr, or close their eyes thereto, that there are serious chances of acci- dent to the lives of human beings and animals from the escapes of electrfcity from open conductors running beneath the roads in connection with the sy-tena they advocate. It is the opinion of many experts that accidents would be even more serious and numerous from underground working than from the overhead trolley sy,tem. It is, of course, impossible to carry on any great public work without attendant accidents, and it really seems that we shall have to content ourselves With the assuranca whici has been given that if iv. compare the t rtal number of passengers carried last year on the overhead electric tramways in tbili country wiO the percentage of accidents, Wd shall find it works out considerably lower than that on the railways and smaller in proportion than the accidents occurring in any ordinary vocation of life. One does not for- get John Bright's twertion that we were safer in the railway train than in the street, and while dis- counting the assurance of the overhead trolley ad- vocate as to the accidents under his system being , "considerably lower" than on railway 4, one is di6- 1 posed to accept that argument as reasonable ant affording some consolation on the whole.

--- A certain Rural District Council in toe West of Englairi, which may as well be nameless. has a re- solution on its books the effect of which is to keep the workmen in its service who are members of any Friendly Society at a disadvantage when overtaken by sickness, as compared with non-members. The plea of the councillors has been that, with their club pay, it was feared when members of these societies were ill, they would be tempted to con- tinue on the " sick list "longer than was considered necessary. But representatives of the Oddfellows and the llsarts of Oak Societies do not accept the plea as sound or sufficient and have entered their protest against it. The point has also received the attention of other Friendly S3cieties, with the re- sult that the district councillors are understood to have re'ented and become willing to have the re- solution rescinded. HORSRMARK7 I'•ST REIT, WARRINGTON.

It would seem to be a wise and an excellent thing I if Boards of Guardians generally would imitate those that have already adapted in workhouses what is knawa as the Brabazon Employment scheme. Judging from the result already attained, the finding by means of this scheme of light occu- pation for aged and infirm inmates, such as knitting I scarves, carving wood brackets, and even painting flowers and Scripture texts, has had the effect of making many an hour pass less tediously and monotously, while it has likewise had a beneficial effect in bringing kind-hearted ladies, who give the instruction, into contact with their pupils; and thus brightenhig the lives of the latter, Sand tending to make them 'sore conteetel. with their lot.

Sr EDWARD'S ANNUAL DANCE

The Public Hall, Runcorn, on Wednesday pre- sented a pretty and lively appearance owing to the elaborate decorations which embellished it. The occasion was the annual dance,in aid of St Edward's Catholic Schools. There was a large and select attendance, and, the floor being in good condition,a programme of twenty-two dances was thoroughly enjoyed. The M.C.'s were Messrs R J Macartney, 11 Prescott, and G Prescott, and the Committee res- ponsible for the very admirable arrangements in- cluded Messrs M Conway, J W Dockery,T Kennedy, R J Macartney, J McCabe, M Nixln, J O'Grady, II Prescottd Winfield, C Watson and J Prescott,whilst the onerous duties of secretary were ably carried out by Mr J Donnellan. The music was supplied by Mr T Knight's Band, Runcorn. During the evening the Revs Father Chambers and Father Brown (War- rington), graced the function with their presence, and the former in a neat speech congratulated the Committee and co-npany on their successful ven- ture.

A RUNCORN BOATMAN " FOUND DROWNED."

Crone t R Dobson, held an inquiry. at the Run- corn Po:ice Court, on Monday afternoon, on the body of Thomas Tying, a boatman. —Jane Ivins identified the body as that of her husband, a boat- man, in charge of the canal-boat "John." On the Saturday evening, witness and her husband hael a quarrel but not of a serious nature. She saw her husband safe on the boat at about 10 to 10 the same evening, but when she returned to the boat a little later, missed him—John Keen, another boatman, testified to learning from Mrs Ivins that her hus- band was missing, and on'finding one of his shoos and cap on the deck of his flat, he grappled for him with a boat-hook. A little lever a tug passed, and intimated that something had been struck by ita propeller. On grappling near the spot deceased's body was recovered.—P.C. George Carrington, gave evidence as to examining the body upon which there was no mark of violence, save a post-mortem one, evidently received from the tug.—A verdict of " Found drowned" was returned.—The Coroner, prior to the inquiry, referred to the late Queen Her reign had been a great and glorious one, and she had left a noble and beautiful example behind her. How mach they regretted her loss he need not say. They were serving that day under King Edward VII, and he felt it was the sincere hope of all that the King would be long spared to them, so that he might follow the splendid example which was her late ilajest3r's most glorious legacy. Mr: James Mousifield, manager to the Boston Deep Sea Fishing and Ice Company, further wrote for the informa- tion of the Board of Guardians, that the term of appren- ticeship for boys was four years, and the form of 'fader* tura the usual one supplied by the Board of Trade. It wae the intection of the C n3pany to provide a home with a Master and Matron to look after them when on shore; also to provide all food and clothing. In addition to this there would also be remuneration according to rank, as apprentice, i e., when he was carried in addition to the ordinary crew he received 61 per week, as deck hand Is. par weekl as third hand it. 61. per week, and " stocker " according to the custom of the pare. Still It would be impossible to give a fixed sum as the remuneration varied with the seasons. Some or their skinp-rs were in receipt, at the pressot time, of £2OO to £3OO per year.§The premium due with each boy was £5, In answer to the Onairman, the Master said the terms appeared to be identical with those of Grimsby. Tnough there did sot seem to ba say home at present; it was prospective.

The Cowman : Yes, the Gthnsby people seem to have a home. The oaly advantage over the Gritnaby paopla seemed to be they Lsed steam trawlers. Tne Adastsr stated the Grimsby C3rnpany also had .tam trawlers._

Mr Ltnaker asked whether the B3ard had any boys at Grimsby. The Master : We have seat several boys there and they have done very well. Mr Baxter contended that the boys did not get as well paid as they would if they worked on farms,and argued that boys of that kind would be better employed on farms. Ha would never dreads of giving a boy is a week for he would be worth 3i or 4 3 a week to any farmer. The Chairman considered they had always in the past acted on that understauuiog, arid tie did not know of one case of a boy going from the house to tho3e people but what they had lost sight of bun. Mr Baxter then intimated he was cognisant of several casos where boys had turned out useful to farmers. The bey was then called in and in answer to the Chairman expressei a desire to go to sea. He also gave an accaunt of his previous employment, and stated that though but 17 years of age he had earned 20s a week " scalling " boilers. This statement caused some astonishment. Mr Linaker, Mr Spruce, Mr Antwiss,and several cellar guardians expressed sympathy with Mr Baxter that suitable boys should if possible be placed with farmers. The Master intimated that the boy had already been refused by farmers, and as both his parent were in the house it was advisable that he should be seat beyond their influence. Subsequently on the motion of Mr Lotwiss, seconded by Mr S Woodward it was decided to leave the case in the Master's bands,and that the b )3,7 be sent to Grimsby if anywhere.

RUNCORN NOTES.

===== 0002601_19010215_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
[BY A COMIESPONDENT.]

That Runcorn Docks could be better protected and lighted, is a truism to which the cold hard facts, as to how the "Sailors' Grave," at Runcorn was opened and filled, can testify with a greater and more eloquent testimony than anything that I could pen. Therefore I leave it, feeling that if the "decaying numbers" cannot move the powers then the task is hopeless.

The inquiry at Runcorn respecting the barrowiog of .£lO,OOO by the District Council for toe Trans- porter Bridge, was of a very formal nature. No objection was raised to the Bridge undertaking, but a serious one was raised to Runcorn's financial position. At a fu'ure date I will undertake to write something of Runcorn's finances; and when the ratepayers can see how in a few years a consider- able amount of loans will have been paid off they will have more faith in their Council, and allow them to go about their business with a little more freedom and a little less bickering,

.£lO,OOO for a bid for freedom! Has anyone realised what the investment of £35,000 by Run- corn and: Widnes in a bridge for intercdmmunica- tion means? L means a3d rate in Rancora,and about a ,I:ttle mare in Widnes. Bucklev-sti:e-ei—Meihodist ree Church.

Oa Sunday Mr W Long worth brought & successful mission to its close at the above plans of worship. The evening service was attended by a large congregation. A farewell tea was given on Monday evening, about 100 partaking of the good things provided. A meeting was subsequently held, the chair being occupied by Council- lor James Evans. The meeting was also addressed by Mr Wibbarley, who paid a tribute to the excellent services renderOd by the missioner. At the close of the meeting Mr John li!ech, secretary of the P SA , presented, on behalf of that society. Mr Longworth with a handsome , framed photograph of himself, as a token of esteem for I the good work done by him for the church during the six weeks' mission, over 100 confessing conversion. Mr Leech expressed the hope that shortly the church would be in a position to make him their pastor. Mr Fairhurst in a few well chosen words on behalf of the church, pre- sented Mr Longworth with a purse of money. The missioner expressed his sincere thanks for the kindness and good wishes expressed towards him. He was much otliged to them for the kind help afforded him during his mission, and trusted that they would continue to do good work.
