WIDNES CRICKET CLUB.

At the Central Hotel on Msnday night the annual meeting of the Widnes Cricket Club was held. There was rather a scanty attendance, but the spirit of the devoted few who put in an appearance more than com- pensated for the lack of numbers. On the proposition ot Dr Edwards, Mr J Smith was voted to the chair. The Secretary, Mr G Owens, reported that the season so far as the matches were concerned had been a very successful one. Out of 17 matches played, ten bad been won, three lost, and four drawn. Toe best averages were Messrs A Callon, 283 runs in 14 innings, an average of 20 21 ; D Jones, 259 in 14 innings, averaee 18.5 ; and B Hampson. 201 in 14 innings, average 12.46. Mr A Callon headed the bowling list with 41 wickete at an expense of 8.53 ; B Hamoson, 30 wickets, 8.96; Dr Edwards, 23 wicket.. 9.43. The Treasurer (Mr It Burrell) reported that the ex- penses together with an overdraft due to the bank of £23 is 7d, which was brought forward, amounted to £l2l is. The inco:ne Was £77 11s 10, including members' subscription% £56. Toe balance at present due to the bank and Treasurer was £2919; 2i ; doubtful debts, £22 lOs ; and arrears in subscriptions, £lO. All the items compared with the previous years were less in proportion, and the club was being worked as economi- cally as possible. The funds were being administered with the greatest possible care, and if they wished to maintain their position in local cricket the management expenses could not be less than last year. They would see that there was a considerable deficit, but he could not think they would allow the oldest institution in the town to he extinguished for want of funds. There was no organization in the town 80 wsrthy of support as the Widnes Cricket Club. After considerable discussion, but with only one. dis- sentient, it Was decided to allow bowling members the privilege of j sining at 51 instead of the usual sum of. 10s 61. Dr Edwards proposed, and it was decided unanimously, to ask Colonel Walker, M.P., for the divi- sion, to become a vice-president. The following officers were elected :—President, Mr F U Gossage ; treasurers, Messrs H Wade Deacon and R Burrell ; captain of the first eleven, Mr J Smith ; sub-captain, Mr D Jones ; captain of the second eleven, Mr G Sown; sub-captain, Mr F Colquett ; secretary, Mr G Owens; general com- mittee : Messrs Callon, H R C olquett, D Lees, R Devonall, Morris Jones, and Upwood. Votes of thanks to the chairman and to the various officers concluded the meting.

STRANGE OCCURRENCE AT

LYMM.

, The prettily situated Cheshire village of Lymm has long enjoyed a high reputation as a healthy and well administered residential district. Within easy distance of Manchester and Warrington, a large number of manu- facturers, merchants, and commercial men generally of theso busy hives of industry turn to this quiet retreat at the close of the day's labour to enjoy its salubrious and bracing air. The numerous palatial residences standing in their own grounds, and the many semi-detached villas along the avenues all bear Evidence of the popu- larity of the district as a residential place. In tbe summer months visitors from the smoky Manufacturing centres of Lancashire pour into the village and take long walks along the dam with its many attractions, or enjoy the pretty scenery associated with its dell. Still with all ite health-giving qualities some residents do not enj y that good health we desire, as a reporter of the' Warrington and Mid-Cheshire Examiner" found,

[From a photo by

tales, Warr ington.]

when he called upon Mrs. Clare, of Sandy-lane, Lymm. Her homestead is really in Ouglyrington, just outside tne village, where the scenery and surroundings are even more r.ral than in the village itself. Mrs Clare has r,sided in the district all her life, and is therefore well known in the locality. Her husband follows the trade of a joiner, and they are the parents of a grown-up tamily of four sons and daughters. Mr. Clare was present at the time the reporter called, and he was able to corroborate the following singular story which was related to the pressman. Up to within a comparatively short time ago Mrs. Clare en- joyed very tolerable health. " Early in the present year," she said "I was suddenly aeiz-d with what I can only describe as nervous fits. The first seizure came on shortly after midnight and strange to say on every succeeding tenth night the , attack was renewed. Each fit rendered me unconscious, and for two or three days after each attack I was un- ab'e to do anything." _ . . .

Mr. Clare here broke in and was able to continue the narrative. " When she was seized with the first tit," he said, " I called in a doctor, and medicine was ministered regularly, regularly, hut in spite of this, a second fit occurred ten days after the first seiz ire, and the second was followed by a third after a like interval of ten days. The attacks became more severe each time." Mrs. Clare then resumed the narration of her ex- perience "I do not know what the end would have been, but one day, which turned out a lucky one for me, I read of a case somewhat similar Co mine which had been cured by Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people. The case was described in a pamphlet which had been pushed under the door, but we had previously read in the newspapers of some wonderful cures by Dr. William' pink pills. My husolnd fetched a box of the pills, aed, I began to take them." " What was the result ?" cperied the reporter. " I casnot say the cure was instantaneous" continued Mrs. Clare. " I took two boxes of Dr. Williams' pills without appearing to derive any benefit from them and for a short interval I did without them entirely. For- tunately, however, I decided to persevere with them and gave them another trial. I took the full dose pre- scribed, three pills at a time thr e times a thy, and I am pleased to say that I have bee i free from fits for over two months now. For many years prior to the fits I was troubled with a pain in my side and that has gone too. I attribute the cure entirely to Dr. Williams' pink pills. lam so pleased with them that I have recom- menAed them to many of my neighbours and friends for various ailments. I have taken over a dozen boxes , altogether and I have to thank Dr. Williams pink pills for enabling me to go about my work again free from the fear of the fits recurring." Mr Clare concurred in what his wife had said, and added that she was unable to do anything for two or three days after each attaek of the fits. Ile was glad his wife had persevered with them for be was sure they bad cured tier.

THE EXAMINER, Pitt n A FEBRIT

NOTES ON (JAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football.

[By " Ovalist.'l

Saturday was a decent sort of football day. It didn't snow, it didn't hail, it didn't rain, and it didn't freeze. In fact it hardly even blew. There was just a slight breeze, about enough to make a kicker imagine that his power has increased all at once, and that was all that was noticeable in the weather line. Most of the atmos- pherical idiosynericies enumerated above had been a feature of the previous few days, cud the turf—or what did duty for the same—was a sufferer in consequence. Grounds were soft—nice to fall on you know—but I rather hampering when speed and agility were required. Some good games were played on Saturday, and not t se worst of them was the one between Warringtou and Leigh at Mather-lane which ended in a pointless draw after a game and exciting struggle.

Sometimes the Warrington team goes to Leigh by horse traction and sometimes by steam traction on the London and North-Western Railway. Which is the speedier method remains open to doubt, and I think the club might advantageously change about every year from road to rail and Ince versa. Then, if there was any superiority about one route over the other the benell;, of it would be obtained at leastionce in every two years. Saturday was one of the Occasions on which the train was selected as the method of locomotion. It is a long way to Leigh—for lame pedestrians—yet the train covered the journey of twelve miles just under the hour, a remarkable performanee even in these days of apesdy travelling. And moreover, the passengers only had to change twice, which is another thing to be thankful for. I And there was no accident en route, which is something I else to be devontly thankful for. One rnieht be killed half a dozen times at least in a twelve-mile tour—" don't you know "

About a hundred enthusiasts followed the fortunes of the Warrington team, some of whom went with the players per train, others " biked," and at least a couple walked. These ardent admirers ot the" wirepullers " thought Leigh had slipped down the map a little and lay about two miles away. Consequently they were a trifle weary on arrival and as three quarter time had elapsed the compensation was slight for the labour undergone. rho exercise was distinctly beneficial, no doubt, and in addition the travellers learnt something of local topography which may come in useful. There is nothing like travel for extending 000'6 views and knowledge.

Leigh have been performing very successfully of la' e but the support they get from the public is very meagre There would not be many more than a thousand specta- tors present on Saturday, and that for a match which generally draws well. If the Warrington crowd did not roll up in larger numbers than ii the case at Leigh, I am afraid Treasurer Pemberton would not wear that smile" So childlike and bland" down at Wilderspoo I on Saturday afternoon. The loyal few at Leigh are strong in the lung, however, and they made their pres- ence heard when anything startling was happening. 'Deere was a quartette party on the stand, two members being of the Warrington male voice choir, and the other couple, Leigh vocalists, The selections were excellent, and particularly noticeable for volume of tone. Certainly they kept things lively, even if the quartette did occa- sionally resemble a local competition oetween Blind Joe and the milkman.

The game its*lf was interesting throughout and the result is a fair indication of the play. Warrington were nearer scoring the oftenest and in fact, ought to have bad a decent margin. but the movements lacked "those finishing touches so essential to success," as one of my contemporaries would say. During .he first half, with the braze behind, Warrington were continuously attacking and had the halves passed with MOTO accuracy points must have accrued. The best I opportunity failed, though, when it looked a certainty for a try. Hoekenhall, Isherwood and Fish held the home full back and his charge at their mercy, but the final pass from the first named went wrong and a heart- felt groan went up from the visiting section of the quartette party. The Warrington left wing was a marked }llan. for Leigh entertained vivid recollections of his three brilliant tries in the previous encounter. Still it was nothing but bad luck which robbed him of at least a couple, for in the second half he had a clear course when the referee brought him back for a torward pass from Isherwood who was impeded when parting with the ball.

With the exception at half.b sok the Warrington team showed a great improvement on previous displays. Burgess was the sinner behind the pack and his reckless passing in the first half last many opportunities. Later on be improved and did seine good work. The three-quarters ' were very go,d and passed exeellently. though the centres did not ,do quite enough breakmg through. Sill there was of touch to touch style of passing. All sh tped ccell, Isher- wood and Harris in particular playing in 7:heir best form. Hallam was excellent, and his plwerful kicking was of great service to WarrMgton as the Leigh cus- todian was not great in the punting line,though remark- ably safe and plucky. Forward, Warrington held the advantage, and to bast such a crack lot as the Leigh eight was a creditable pea prm snce. The two Askam recruits played with plenty of dash and their work in the pack was apparent in results. The forward ranks should be equal to ;anything when the injured men are available once more.

On the Leigh side W Pearson (half) and Towers (full back) were the best performers, Dunbavin being only moderate. Kight and Ganloy on the wing did excellent service, the former watching Fish like a oat. Be is a grand tackler too. MacSl asters, the vet.ran, was the pick of the forwards.

Warrington get a Point at Leigh.

The first meeting this season between the Leigh and Warrington clubs came off at Leigh on Saturday in the presence of about 2,000 'people. Bath teams were strongly represented, the 'chosen fifteen turning out in each Case. In contrast to the previoui S sturdays, the weather was fine, though the ground was decidedly on the soft side in cmsequence of the rain and snow and frost which had been so prevalent, ?Jr F Kennedy, of Broughton, was the referee and promptly at 3 p.m. the teams lined out in the following order. Firemen Spend a Pleasant Evening. By the kind permission of the Fire Engine Com- mittee the annual dinner in connection with the brigade was held at the Fire Station on Saturday evening. • The msn sat down to a very substantial meal, ably catered for by Mrs Thomas Turner. After- wards a very pleasant evening was spent, songs and recitations being given by various members. During the evening Councillor Smethurst called, and his health and that of Superintendent Turner were drunk with musiral honours. The Chairman spoke of the excellent manner in which the brigade had discharged their duties during the past twelve months. He also gave some of his own reminis- cences of fires. The Superintendent suitably re- sponded. The brigade desire to thank all subscribers who contributed to the evening's enjoyment.

IT is said of a gentleman that he had a passion for the purchatie of second-hand furniture at auctions, and that, in making "good bargains," he had filled his house with antiquated and almost Useless articles. Upon one occasion, his wife took the responsibility, without consulting or apprising her husband, to have a portion of the least useful removed to an auction-room. Great was her dis- may, and her extreme astonishment, when, on the evening of the day of sale, a majority of the articles Came back to the house. The husband had stumblik into the auction-room, and, not knowing kis ow' fnaniture, had purchasod it at better bargains that at first.

MR. A. H. CROSFIELD AT THE LIBERAL CLUB.

Distribution of Billiard Handicap

Prizes.

M

An enjoyable smoking co ecert was provided in the Reading Room at the Warrington Liberal Club on Thursday last week in connection with the annual distribution of prizes gained in the billiard handi- cap. Mr. A. H. Crosfield, the esteemed president, of Ithe club, occupied the chair, and amongst those Ipresent were Councillors linnion and Plumpton, Mr J E Warren, Mr J Critchley, Mr Locker' Mr Bibby, Mr Hibbert, Mr Ford and Mr Thomas Pem- berton (secretary). Dr R Smith wrote apologising for his non-attendaace. An excellent programme lof vocal and instrumental items was sub- mitted, and the efforts of the artistes were very successful. The solos by Messrs Woods Hassell and Gough were given in such an lexcellent manner as to secure enthusiastic encores. Mr W Whittaker delighted the audience with his inimitable whistling solos and Mr F Davies proved himself a beautiful executant on the oboe. The humour of the evening was supplied by Mr H Price and Mr E A Sanders. The former contributed comic songs and Mr Sanders gave some h amorous sketches which kept the audience in roars of laughter. The duties of accompanist were efficiently discharged by Mr W Critchley. During an interval in the programme Councillor Tinnion stated that it had been suggested that he should propose a vote of thanks to Mr A H Crosfield for his generosity in presenting the billiard handl- ' can prizes. The members of the club took a keen in- terest in the billiard handicaps, and he was sure that that was not only a source of pleasure to the committee, but also to Mr A El Crosfield. (Hear, II hear). The handicap in question was a record one lin point of numbers for it was taken part in by 140 members as against 130 in the handicap that preceded it. The happiest relations existed between the members of the club and the committee generally. There was never a time when the committee worked harder for the beet interests of the club and the happiness and convenience of the members, and hei,believed there was never a time when the member a so cordially appreciated the disinterested labours of the committee. (Hear, hear.) Apart from that they had an,excellent presi- dent— (applause)—a gentleman who was prepared and who was also willing to do all he possibly could to further the best interests of the Liberal Club and the Liberal party in Warrington. (Hear, hear.) Unless Mr Crosfield had come forward with his usual generosity they would not have had the excellent prizes which they now saw. He (Councillor Tinniun took a special interest in this year's prizes because he happened to be one of the winners. (Applause.) He was afraid that increasing years had a tendency to decrease the quality of his billiard play,but still he persevered, and like a good many others, whether he won or lost, he enjoyed the game In the recent handicap they had some splendid games and the interest in the game tended very largely to help the social element of the club. Billiards were only another form of education. The game demanded patience, tacc,a bit of cunning, and above all, perseverance and care, and if they only applied these good qualities to their every-day work they would be all the better' men for it. (Applause). Councillor Plumpton seconded the vote of-thanks and said that with respect to these gatherings they had to thank their president for the occasion of them. He did not think there wai a man anywhere who took a greater interest in the recreation and in the interests of the working-classes than Mr A H Crosfield did. (Hear, hear.) He had done much to keep alive an interest in the Liberal Club, and every member thanked him for his assistance. They hoped they might have Mr Crosfield with them many years to come and that they might yet see him in a more prominent position than he at present occupied. .(Applause.) He (Councillor Plumpton) felt sure that the day would come. (dear, hear.) He did not want to talk politics but he ventured to express the opinion that at the present time the trade of Warrington was not represented in Parlia- ment, but it they could return Mr Crosfield the trade and commerce of the town and the interests of working classes in general would be properly represented. (Hear, hear.) 'rhe proposition was then put to the meeting and carried with enthusiasm.

Mr A H Crosfield, who on rising to reply was heartily cheered, said he had given a litila subscrip- tion to these prizes; that was his share in the proceedings, He hoped be might be allowed to say that what Councillor Plumpton had told them of his (Mr Crosfield's) interest in the relaxations of the working-classes and the middle-classes, so far as he had been able to assist them or forward them, had always afforded him an unmixed pleasure. There were many questions on which it was rather difficult to arrive at as just and wise conclusion, but about this healthy relaxation for those who had to do the hard work of the world, whether it be indoor relaxation or out- door relaxation, it Was iMpOQSibis to have two opinions. (Hear, hear.) It had alwaysibeen a great pleasure to him to participate in a small way in furthering the interests of sport and the interests of gam. es, partly because one liked to see other people enjoy themselvee,jast as you liked to enjoy yourself and partly because he came of a family which had always taken a pretty keen interest in games. Sometimes they saw people shaking their heads and saying that the English people were rather too much given to games. Of course there Was moderation in all things and we had got to work with our brains and our muscles, and put our best foot foremost, there was no doubt, if we were to bold our own in the competition of the present day. Personally he did not keep his nose to the grindstone quite as close as he did (hear, hear and laughter)—but still the major por- tion of his life was wrapped up in the trade with which he wasUssociated. and for which he had been and still was, the honorary secretary. He had seen good deal of the pressure and competition that existed in trade and it had been a very great lesson to him. Most emphatically he believed, we were not going to succeed any better, whether as direc- tors,*managers, employers foremen or workmen, if we left behind us the old traditions of Englishmen who had been natty with their hands and clever with their games and who had been better men 1 better workers and he contended,the better citizens for it. (Applause.) He thought there was one other feature that was worth alluding to, These games, whether it be football, cricket, billiards or golf—(A Voice : "Bowls")—yes and bowls—brought them to- gether very pleaeantly—(hear, bear)—and like music and other civilising influences, helped to ' perpetuate a good feeling between the different classes of the people and that was a very necessary thing in the years that were before us (Applause.) If there were time and if that were the occasion, perhaps one might be inclined to do what one had done before, and that was to intersperse a few re- marks upon politics, but he thought they had better reserve any politics for another occasion. In conclusion Mr Crosfield alluded to the approaching visit of Stevenson to the Liberal Club to play an exhibition game of billiards, and faceti, ously remarked that he (Mr Crosfield) would have to firid time to work up what little faculty he had for the game of billiards because their excellent secretary wrote him a letter that rather took his breath away. In fact he told him he would have to , see what he could do in playing this successor to 'Roberts. (Hear, hear.) In an interesting way, Mr Crosfield proceeded to refer to a game be had with Gaskell, whom he considered the most artistic and scientific amateur he had ever seen handle a cue, a gentleman who beat Fry the only time he played him for the amateur championship of England. He thought it was a very good idea to get Steven- son down. He was quite sure they would have a great treat in watching Stevens3n, and no one would be more pleased than himself, even if he (Mr Crosfield) was beaten by 799. (Laughter). Mr. Crosfield then proceeded to distribute the prizes, and congratulated the winners. He hoped the club would contitie to beat records. (Appleuse). The following received prizes :-Ist prize, flower stand, Thomas Maguire; 2nd, fern pot lamp, J Jennings ; 3rd, case of carvers, A .1 Jones ; 4h, tea- pot, R smith ; sth, cruet, W Woods ; 6th, clock. iLttters to the ebttor.

Suggested Queen Victoria Memorial

in Warrington.

Sir,—lt the present time when so many of our prin- cipal towns are considering the most fitting way to commemorate the memo.y of our late beloved Queen Victoria, I have wandered how we in this town could rise to the occasioo,and it has occurred to me that some scheme might be formulated to give everyone an oppor- tunity of contributing. The population of Warrington being about 60,000 I would suggest that 60.000 shilliags be subscribed, th is one shilling for each person, that a Roll be opened in which every subscriber be entitled to sign their name and when completed deposited in the Warrington Museum. This would make a total of £3.000 with which I rug- ge t the authorities of the Ihfirmary be asked to set apart a ward to ho called " The Queen Victoria Me- morial Ward." It is well known our departed Queen was interested in these noble institutions and I trust this or some similar scheme will:appeal to the hearts of all our loyal townspeople.

F. PENDLEBURY,

===== 0002601_19010215_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
Holly Villa, Bewsey t. oad. The United Mission of the Free Churches.

Sir,—May I venture, through your kinclaees, to in- vite the attmtion of our readers of your widely circul- ated journal to the Special Evangelistic Mission which Is to be opened on Sunday in this town It may be explained that this mission is psrt of a simultaneous movement that will begin on Sunday in nearly or quire all the cities and town, of this country. Not only is the movement simultaneous, it is also united. All the Free Evangelical Churches in the country are combined in the pro- motion of this great endeavour for the furtherance of religion. The scheme has been originated by the Column of the National Federation of the Five Churches, and is being heartily and unanimously supported by the leaders, both lay and ministerial, of the various Evan- gelical Denominations. Never before has so broad and uaited an effort been attempted, or oven devised. The conviction now etiolating the Fres Churches is that their points of difference are absolutely nothing in coa- -1 parison with their infinitely more important points of agreement. Sectarian aims, and preferences of ecclesi- astical policy, are being fergotten in the intense desire to eommend the Gospel to the people, and to commence this New Century by a loud, and earnest and unani- mous call upon (Jur fellow countrymen to consider the Sovereign claims of God and the urgent and paramount needs of their own highest welfare. There is no need for me to state here the details of the Mission arrangements, as they appear in your adver- tising columns. I may however,make a brief, but nine the less cordial, reference to the Missioner, the Rev. John Gould, of Cambridge. This gentleman comes to the Mission upon the suggestion of the National Connell, which has taken utmost pains to select and suggest able and experienced Misssioners to the several localities. Mr Gould, is I understand, a stranger to this town, but up- on his visit several weeks ago to meet the workers who will assist him in this Mission. he created the happiest impression, and we are confidently anticipating that both his words and his methods will meet with general and cordial acceptance. My desire in this letter is to ask for the Mission the interest and the sympathy of your readers of all sects and parties. It is encouraging to know that when, the week before last, this movement was commenced in London it created very great interest both in the Press and among the people generally. The Inaugural Service was held, by special permission of the City Authorities, In the Guildhall, and was attended in state by the Lord Mayor and Sheriffs, and by large numbers of the leading citizens. Throughout the country, interest is being awakened, and local leaders have been encouraged in many instances by words.! grsod will from clergy and members of the Established Church. It is being widely recognized that the movement is not one of self-intereit or of Nonconformist or Denominational motive, but one that is entirely Christian and patriotic. And therefore, Sir, I venture to appeal to your readers and to yourself to aid us in this movement in every way that may be possible. On behalf of the Elecutive Committee of !ha War- rington Free Church C•uncil, I am. yours faithfully, JOHN YONGE, President. February 14th, 1901.

WIDNES AND RUNCORN BRIDGE . COMPANY, LIMITED.

The first meeting of the shareholders of this company, which has been formed for the purpose of constructing a transporter bridge across the river Kelsey between Widnes and Runcorn, was held on Monday afternoon. at the offices of the company, in Liverpool. Sir John Brunner, Bart., M.P., chairman of the company, occu- pied the chair, and there were present Mr 11 Setoo-Karr, M.P. Mai J W Wareing, Mr Holbrook Gaskell ion, J.P., Alderman H S Timmis,J.P., directors ; Messrs. Oppenheim and Alsop (solicitore), Stables (secre- AM). J J Webster, M.I.C,E. (joint engineer),R Garnett, R H Poluett, D M'Kechnie. R L Neilson, W Handley, J.P., F J Poole, J Mercer, G J Rimmer jun., and other shareholders. The Chairman informed the shareholders that the whole ot the capital had • been subscribed, and that the directors had spent Mout £4OO in advertising, issniog of prospectus, Zre., in order to raise the capital. The Local Government Board had arranged to hold local ioquiries this week, at Runcrrn on Thursday and at Widnes en Friday, into the applications of the local authorities of those districts for sanction to borrow money wherewith to inwnt in that company (Widnes £25 000 and Runcorn £10,000). Be had no fear that serious obj-ction would be raised by toe rat payers of either town to the action of their councils. The two local authorities were heartily with them, and he thought that the whole of the public of Lancashire and Cheshire were satisfied that they had undertaken a piece of work tbat would he greatly to tile advantage of the two counties, especially of those two important towns—Widnes and Ruecorn. The Board had instructed their engineers, Wain Webdter and Wood, to proceed with their:working drawings, which would probsb'y be ready within three months. The Chairman proposed that the number of directors be increased to nine, remarking that this was done to enable the Widnes Town Council to have two representa- tives and the Runcorn Urban District Council one repro. sentative on the board. Mr Seton-Karr, M.P., seconded, and the motion was carried. Sir J T Brunner, Batt., M.P., Mr II Seten-Karr, M.P., Mr D Wade Deacon, J.P.,C.4.. Mr Flollarook Gaskell, Jun., J.P., Alderman H S Jimmie, LP., and Major J W Wareing were unanimously appointed directors, and Messrs J W Davidson and B Cookson were appointed auditors.

THE GREAT FREE CHURCH

MISSION.

Runcorn.

From February 16th to February 28th next, the Free Church Council of Runcorn will ho!d a simultaneous mission at Su Paul's Church, Runcorn. For weeks past, and particularly since the commencement of the New Century, the pastors from their respective rostrums have told the people that s great work was about to be com- menced, having for its object the furthering of" the Master's coming," "For He shall come when the harvest is ripe," and how better can we ripen tbe hazy' •; than by the nurturing and fostering, the tendering and we- l- ing of the plants we see around U 9. Work of this kind can alone 10 accomplished by the missioner, and the great question with us shnuld be, are we going to aid in the great undertaking ? How often have we not satirically put it : " Talk of missions abroad ; there is more need for them at home ! " And now we have one within cur midst are we going to withhold ? To stand aloof and g.tza upon others struggling with a task to great for them, whilst the very aid we refine is all that hinders their success, would be a reproach to us for ever. The Rev Alfred Wells (St. ) se:11 officiate as , missioner, and such is the tur.n of the miresters of the other Free Clourches,that they have with one accord; worthy of the great cause, consented to act es his assistants. Another happy augury I for a successful campaign against sin, lathe fact thitt the choirs of the churcheo are perfectly united. Thus with one heart the " Peals° of the Lord " may (mend ; whilst, amid the congregation, the sweetest strains of lovely mission hymns may soften the hearts and cause many cords of memory to bring back the soul to God: The order of service is :—Sundav s, 10.20, 2.30, and 6 0 week nights, 7 15 ; noon-day prayer meetings, 12.30 to 1 ; afteiiloon Bible readings, 3to 4 o'clock. spEeil meetings will also be held as follows :—Workers Meet- ing, Saturday Februo, ; 113th at 7, (Administration of the Lord's Supper), Young Peoples' S-rvice, Sunday afternoon, February 17th at 2 30; Service for Wonaea only, Monday afternoon, February 18th, in Brunswieb: School, at 230; Set vice for men only, Sunday afternoon. February 24th, in Bethesda Church, at 230. A hearty welcome is extended to all. Hymn books will be pro- vided, and everything done to make us realise we are hound together in one great Christian Bond. For par ieutars of pulpit changes &c., see programmes.

Battle With De Wet.

A " Lond on Evening News " telegram states that Boers under De Wet entered Cape Colony and occupied P hillipstown on Wednesday. They were attacked by British and driven out of the town with loss.

The " Fighting Fortieth" at Spion Kop. A Warrington Sergeant's Bravery.

Included in the long list of officers, nos-com- missiened officers, and men brouzbt to the netice of the Government by General Buller for distin- guished and gallant service in the Ladysmith opera- tions are the following : Distinguished Conduct,

let Batt. South Lancashire Regiment.—Sergeant Price, January 24, Spion Kop ; Conspicuous bravery in controlling his section under very heavy fire,and as a volunteer, traversed 200 yards under a very heavy fire to assist a signaller Private Brighouse, February 27: Displayed great coolness and re- source under heavy flre,and when severely wounded in the side insisted on removing his own accoutre- ments and attempted to dress his own wound while remainder kept up rifle fire. Sergeant Price ia a Warrington man, and previous to his leaving for the front with the South Lanca- shire reservists, was employed at the works of Messrs Pearson and Knowles. The recognition of his gallantry has given great satisfaction to his numerous friends, Special Mention.

Sir Redvers calls attention to other officers, non— commissioned officers, and men whose services de- serve " special mention." The list includes Second- Lieu. C H Marsh, Ist Batt. S. Lancashire Regiment. "The gallant conduct of this 'young 'officer on February 27th was brought specially to my notice. After his company commander was wounded he behaved in a most gallant manner, encouraging his men, who were exposed to a heavy shrapnel, machine-gun, and rifle fire. A wound which he had received on January 23rd bad broken out and was bleeding." Gallant and Meritorious Conduct.

ST. HELENS.

A WELL-KNOWN SWIMMER AND MIS WIFE. At St. Helens Police Court, on Monday, Robert Sher rock. of Ashcroft-street, St. Helens, the well-known swimmer and international water-polo player, was sum- moned for deserting his wife, Alice. Mr H L Riley appeared for the complainant and Mr E W Swift for ths defendant.—Mr Riley stated that the defendant had dis- tinguished himself as a swimmer and water-polo player. Two years ago he joined the White Star Club at Liver pool, and obtained wort under the White Star Com- pany, where he got good wages. They took a house at Bootle and lived comfortably far some time. Five weeks ago he called in a broker and sold up the house for £lO, out of which be gave her £4 lea and told her she could Ilko where she liked as he was returning to his mother's house at St. Helens. Mrs Sharrock followed him to St Helens, and the outcome was those proceedings.—Mr Swift applied for an adjournment on the ground that the defendant had offered to get work and take his wife bask again.—Mr Riley opposed, and &eked for an ender of maintenance uprm the defendant—The bench granted a generation. and ordered the defendant to pay 10,1 per week to his wife.

THE PASSING OF THE QUEEN.

(Specially Written for the ‘` Examiner.")

At a little before noon on Friday, the Ist of Feb- ruary, in company with some two hundred of the busy folk who serve as eyes and ears to all the multitudes less fortunate, I sailed from Portsmouth to behold the stateliest and most imposing pageant which the world has ever seen. Our vessel—specially chartered for us by the Admiralty, and called by the same name as that which was to bear the body of the Queen across those narrow waters for the last time—silently dropped from tl,e quay. and past the " Victory " and the " St. Vincent" —whose very names made music in our hearts— and by the shining Southsea beach, and out into the glittering Solent, and then turned northwards through the mighty lane of battleships which stretched in two great Itn4s until they vanished from our sight amid the mist which still hung whitely over Cowes. Upon the right,the formidable armament began with the " Majestic," and on the lett —mute witnesses of world-wide smypathy— there lay four German warships, and a big French vessel, an I the mighty " Flatsuse," with the sunrise banner of Japan flung out above its stern, and said to be the largest ironclad afloat ; and, last of all, the ships of Portugal and Spain, We dropped our anchor in a breach reserved for us in the great iron well, just opposite the " Niobe,' whilst on our lee the spires and roofs of Ryde loomed out amidst the silver mist like some vague phantom city. Beyond, there stretched another line of battleship;; above. the sky was blue as unstained sapphire; below, the sea as bright as emerald ; and pre-ently, the wintry sun, ascending higher, drove off the trailing curtains of the mist, and showed us all the spreading limbs of Portsmouth and its suburbs, and the gentle hills of Rampshire, and the grim forts whica guarded its historic harbour, and upon the west, the smiling slopes and vnois and meadows of the little island where, beneath its two white towers that crowned a green and gentle undulation, lay the body of the dear, dead Quoen ! Beyond the head- land, hidden from our view, the little town of Cowes was basking in the February sunshine, and thence, soon after noon, the body wou'd be rave - ently borne to the "Alberta,' and h-tween those ocean monsters, pass to Gosport and the funeral train, Upon those twin towers standing out so peacefully above the grassy slopes the eyes of all the world were turned, whilst the quays of Hyde the forts of Portsmouth and the miles and miles of yellow sand were black with the unnumbered thousands who had come, from near and far, to watch the west Queen start upon the last journey home.

The red and white Cape liner, " Scot," which bore I the members of the House of Common, presently sailed slowly past us and let fall her anchor, jut beyond, and soon a smaller steamship, the "Dun- vegan Castle," joined us, with her stately load of peers. A. quiet hour or more of meditation followed, and at last the hour arrived at which the melancholy cortege was to leave for Cowes. A I dead hu+h fell up3n us all, and we could almost fancy that we heard the wailing of the Highland 'pipers as the sad procession started down the sunny slopes towards the pier, And suddenly there came la dull, hoarse boom from somewhere just Feyond I the headland, and soon another and another, and now there flashed, in turn,from every iron monster's side, a sudden lightning, and there curled a wreath of silver smoke, and boom, boom, boom, there came the thunder of the guns all down the uouble line of battle-ships that stretched from Cowes to Ports- mouth. And we looked into each other'a faces with dim eyes, and held our breath,for we knew that the procession was at last upon its way. In front of us, upon the" Niobe," the sailors stood in ilark blue ranks, as mute as statues, tbe marines—a streak of scarlet—lending colour to a sombre spectacle repeated right along the line. Each man wit h arms reversed, each ship with banners half- mast high ; and every minute once again there came the distant lightnings, and the sudden thunder, passing down the lane of battleships in dull rever- berating echoes till it died away by distant Southsea beach.

The minutes crawled along on leaden feet, and it seemed an age before we saw, far off, eight low, black grim " destroyers," gliding in a double line, with; slow and solemn motion, down this lane of battleship. and then, behind them—oh, how the vision thrilled us all!—the boat that bore the Queen! The guns boomed on, the black crowds on the dis- tant shores strained eyes and hearta to hear and see; and now, amid the death-like mist, there came the wail of funeral music from the silent battleships,' and every head was bared, and every eye grew dim: for yonder, underneath that white and purple nail, the crown of gold above it shining in the sun. there I lay the Rnapre,s of the Seas I Like some majestic scene in shadowland, amid a silence wily broken by the dull boom of the guns, and by the heart-break- ing wail of the music, the vessel neared. and reach- ed, and passed us, and we hushed our very hearts, and only breathed again when it had gone, and in its wake, mystical with bannere streaming proud- ly out from every mast —in symbol of eternal sovereignty—the ship which bore the King, the Queen,the Kaiser. Then came Vie"Osborne,"—with more royal mourning—and behind her loomed up now the great white " rlohenzollern," the Kaiser's noole yacht, her flag, like all save those upon the boat which which bore the king, suspended half mast high. The Admiralty yacht"Rochantress,"and the Trinity yacht" Irene ' , closed the sad proces- sion ; but our eyes still followed that far glimpse of white and gold, and only rested when the booming of the guns behind her gralually died away, the distant forte took up the weird salute in turn, and soon the line of ships was lost amid the purple mists of Portsmonth Harbour. Meanwhile, the sun was sinking in a blaze of gold above the spires and towers of Ryde, and over all the hills and woods and meadows to the north; and now its last rays rested like some shining aureole above the towers of Osborne, and the glassy waves became a gold en pathway to the dead Queen's island home, and all the sky grew luminous with unimaginable hues, and all the battle-ships were touched with a celestial glory; and, as presently, we too turned silently into its purple harbour, whilst the sun sank down, a ball ef living splend- our in the west ; and from the east, with white, sad face, the moon crept up to bid the Queen goo-bye, There name from some dim tower amid the purple mist across the harbour the etheral music of such bells as might t ave been the very music of the bells of Heaven. A boatman rowed me, in the gathering darkness, round the shadowy inlet, by the dear old "Vic- tory," whose guns had boomed the last salute, and near the "Osborne," and beneath the bows of the King's boat, and just below the mighty " Ilohenzallern," where the flag was floating at the masthead,for the Kaiser had but lately gone aboard whilst, far away amidst the darkness of the dock where shs was m o o r the royal yacht, " heeir Alberta," where, with gallant Queen seamen guarding he pointed the lights upon the would rest uutil P o she started on her last great journey through her hushed and avve.struck capital. Soon after eight o'clock I joined in the great black human line which, with sombre tributaries pouring in from every street, was rolling rapidly down Holborn towards the West. Diverging pre- sently, I reached " V-ctoria Station " by another route. The approaches were already cloned en every side by stalwart ranks of stern policemen,and I had to exercise a good deal of diplomacy to pass the formidable column ; but succeeding, found myself at a few minutes to nine, in the front row of the extreme edge of the mighty crowd— some millions strong—which stretched in two unbroken lines from here to Paddington I was in full view of the station, and the great broad roae. which flanked it on the right—a wonder- fully fine position ; but there were still above two hours to wait, and the cold wind sweeping past the station cut one like a knife. Continually the crowds increased, and very aeon the roads were closed, and all the etands, and all the windowed ay, and all the roofs and towers, in sight grew black with a great sorrow-stricken multitude, Majestic purple draperies adorned the leading buildings and green wreaths ef laurel hung gag- geatively from well-nigh every lamp along the route, and everywhere huge flags were floating half-mast high, and not a shop was there which had not closed its doors ; but most of all it touched me to perceive how even the very poorest of the poor had scraped their hard-earned pence together and turned out in mourning garb. Policemen lined each side of the road which led into the station, and soon the Grenadiers in jet-black belmeta and gray overcoats marched up, and took their places just in front of 119. And now so closely was the cordon drawn, that folks with seats in the Hotel just opposite for which high prices had been paid were absolutely forbidden to cross the road. And we forgot the biting wind in watching regi- men' ,' of every sort and kind ride proudly by to talw their places in the van of the procession,which

extended right away to ficcedilly. Amongst them i we were especially attranted by a little group of Highland pipers and the Irish Guards—that recent m inument of the dead Queen's wisdom—whilst a gallant company ot Bluejeckete marched past with svvinging stifle, to disappear within the station,and glittering Lancere,aud stern-faced Hussars and eta!- wart Guardsmen, and "Soldiers of the Queen " of every sort and kind meved by in picturesque array their banners—blazoned with world famous victories—concealed in crape, and their dashing uniform, completely hidden by their heavy over- coats, whilst offieere with shining orders on their manly breasts, ant crape binds on their arms, and waving plumes and clattering of swords swept past us to the etetion, and, at last ; a dull reer surged along the crow led 'thoroughfares, and from the hushed and sorrow-stricken throng there (Male the murmur, "Bob !" It was the only cheer I heardithat day,and it was muffled like the bele which over all the world had tolled a knell for the departed. But who could check the tribute to the gallant little gray-haired general with the grave fees and the ruddy cheeke, or watch him ride up quietly with- out'e thrill of pride? A brief word with the mounted officers in front of us, and then he disappeared to join the company of king!, and princes, and ambient lore, and potentates from every realm who passed us in increasing crowds to swell the greet assensblege of distinguished folks beside the surreptu- one white and purple tent which Blundell Maple's men were richly furnishing as I returned the pre- vious night from Porternouth. And soon the golden earriages with scarlet drivers and resplendent footmen came, and then—whilst every voice , grew mute—a khaki-cavered gun carriage 'drawn by eight cretin-coloured ponies! The clock above tne station pointed t 3 eleven now and. shortly afterwards, we heard the puffing of an engine and exclaimed instinctively: " The train has come !” The silence deepened as we waited, and the minute, seemed like hours; the towering Grenadiers reversed their arms, and leaned upon them with bowed beads like living menuments of grief, and soon a mounted soldier at the angle in the roadway raised a flag and signalled to some other meunted soldier, who, in turn, palmed on the message te the next, and so it sped like light— ning righ-• along the route; and then—with a heart piercing pathos which will haunt me till I too have passed into the darkness—there came the muffled roll of drama, and the melodious wail of Chopin'i thrilling Feneral March, and at the sound the hairy helmets a'l began to move ; with sow, sad feet the endleem ranks of soldiers marched away ; and ws knew that the procession had started. I shall never forget the pathos of that solemn moment. Strong men bit their lips to hide their feelings, and many could not way their tears. But look l—a blaze of generals is moving by—smongst them "Bob," and Evelyn Wood, and lan Hamilton, and General Brackenbury ; and after them the sallow- faced, black-bearded Duke of Norfolk, premier nobleman of England, who had left the Cabinet to seize the sword and fight his country's battles over sea, and now, as the Earl Marshal had been called upon to organise the most impressive spectacle in England's history, Behind him came Gold Sticks, White Staves, and glittering aides-de-camp—amongst them some of England's greatest noblsmee—and then, big' u., upon the gun carriage, and plainly visible to every- body in the crowd, the collie covered with its white and purp'e pall, and, shining out upon it in the pale gleam of a sudden glimpse of wintry sun, the greatest crown the world has ever gazed upon, the golden orbs and sceptre of the grandest sovereignty in all " the course of time," above the still form of the woman most beloved of all the women in the , world. And every head was reverently bared, and every tongue grew dumb with a great awe as very quietly the coffin with its nrecious load went slowly ' by. Behind it came the King—erect an,l dignified, and riding—on a noble horse—s splendid specimen of English manliness: his one gallant brother on hi a left, and on his right—a pale, grave, thoughtful figure with a black moustache—a perfect type of regal dignity—the Balser! A steed grew restive as the King rode .3 y. He paused immed- iately in front of us, and very calmly —but in a voice which everybody could distinctly bear—he said : " Will some one take that officer's horse?" It was a trivial incident, but it was typical of the unfailing tact and self- possession which will help to make the seventh Edward one of the most useful Kings who have ever filled our English throne. The sequel was lost to me, for the procession still moved slowly on, and King and Keiser turned to follow it towards the palace. Behind them came the Master of the florae the King of Greece, the King of Portugal, and a dazzling line of princes representing practically every leading nation in the world ; and then, in great grey cloaks, a number of officers of the Ger- man Army and the members of the Kaiser's suite, and then five glittering state carriages from one of which the sweet, sad, pale face of the Queen looked forth, her daughters by her side, and in the next the bearded monarch of the Belgians, accompanied by the King's sisters, and in the next the gray old Duke of Cambridge, with Prince Edward of Saxe- Weimar, and Lord Wolseley. The last, two carriages eontained the ladies of the household and in one -of them there also sat Lord Law- rence, and in the other Lord Churchill. Still more grey-coated Germans [followed, and last of all, in long, loose, fluttering scarlet cloaks, the gallant Life Ouards came!

And, as the black awl silent multitu le began to move and speak again, and a stalwart body of policemen marched away to go by special train to Windsor how I wished that I could have been a policeman myself for a couple of hours or eel I hurried to the Underground and booked for Praed street. I got to Paddington, as I aoticipated, well in advanca of the procession, But the road between the two stations was kept by 'mounted lancers, and the crowds were so immense that, after vainly seeking to make re y way to the front, I quietly crept out again, and round by a back street until I reached the point atiwhich the funeral cortege ma4e its final turn. Here too both windows, paths, and gardens were completely occupied by the enormous crowd. and the very trees were black with human fruit, whilst purple draperies adorned the houses every- where, and loyal hearts and eyes looked out from every window. And here I saw the changing hel- mets of the various famous regiments which headed the great retinue, and heard once more the haunt- ing wail of that melodious music, and again, above the black and silent crowd, beheld the last remains of earthly sovereignty move slowly by beneath its pall of purple and of gold, behind it Kings and Kaisers awed and voiceless in the presence of the greater King called Death I I saw, the Royal Train and beyond the gateways of the station—access to which was absolutely barred—and from the bridge beyond it heard the funeral music wailing out upon the wintry air again, and caught the dull puff of the Royal Engines, and the slund of rolling wheels; and I knew that Queen Victoria had left her mighty capital for the last time! The earliest train to Windsor did not start till half-past three, and, just as I arrived, the funeral guests were beginning to return. The Royal Train was drawn up in the station the Saloon which bore the coffin, having been detached at Slough. For close upon an hour the various platforms were kaleidoscopic in their shifting maze of shining uni- forms, and princes, peers, ambassadors, and poten- tates of every rank and realm walked by in twos and threes to take their seats in one or other of the four great trains which had been specially provided. In one compartment I could see the Primate, pale, and calm, and thoughtful, in another, with three ladies in the deepest mourning, I descried the Premier, leaning wearily back in the corner by the window, the great black beard which I so well re- membered growing very grey, in yet another, Mr Goschen sat reflectively—the man to whom the Queen had mainly owed her proudest title, " Mis- tress of the Seas." Presently up strolled Earl Spen- cer, his red beard, too, becoming white, alas The Duke of Norfolk bustled here and there about the platform, evidently very busy, and anon a brilliant group of Privy Councillors would stride majestically by, the gorgeous "Beef-eaters," in all the splendour of their gold and scarlet, pass into the train, the Chinese Ambassador stalks stolidly to his compart- ment, and a little Prince from far Siam discovers that he has been contemptuously relegated to an ordinary waiting room! Meantime, the Royal train itself was drawn up at the further platform, opposite the Royal Wait- ing Room—its rich interior beautiful with flowers —and in the broad approaches opposite, a guard of honour stOod like statues waiting till the Kaiser came. And presently the great grey-coated German giants who had come from Portsmouth with us on the previous night, and formed an interesting feature in the day's procession, walked Leong the platform, took their seats, and evidently brought the news that their great lord and master had elec- ted to remain behind. And then the last train, with its load of notables, steamed slowly out, the guard of honour left, an army of policemen marched into

the station ready to go back to town,and gradually the vice resumed its normal aspect. I dined at tthe Castle Hotel, and strolling after- wards about the anciew; streets and underneath the frowning walls, heart how enormous crowds hal finked into the town whose purple windows every- where proclaimed its woe ; how some of them had , actually slept all night in the Park, how, just outside the Station, the steeds which were ,to draw the coffin through the town had sullenly refused to stir; and how the King, as if by inspiration, had suggested that the Blue- jackets should take their places,and so the " Handy gala" had crowned his countless services to his devoted country by dragging the body of his Sovereign up the Castle Hill for the last rim.. F-ont guardsmen at the gates, from humble shopkeepers, from everyone I met, I heard the same tale of the dead Queen's kindliness, and how the Windsor people loved her as a fziend And then, azirl the gathering darkness, I strolled up the slope towards the gates, and past the sentries and police, SO right into the very precincts of the private portion of the Castle. where the lights were blazing in the chamber occupied by kings and queens, but would not shine again upon the queenliest of them all. A gossip with a stalwart Guardsman and an ancient, servant of the Royal Household, and I passed into St. Gerrze's Chapel. Right round it gray stone walls I noticed endless wreaths, and" sauntering through the purple nave—where several workmen stood—and through the dimly-lighted choir, where an official eyed me curiously, I came upon still richer wreaths in endless sequence, and, at last, amid the darkness, reached a great, strong, Olken door. ehinl it I could hear vague voices talking in an undertone. They were the voices of the two tall Guardsmen who were standing, with fixed bayonets, upon the threshold of that inner shrine where, reverently watched both night and day by six high offieers, the dead Queen lay, within a silence which no prying feet might penetrate, no curious voices of the'outside world disturb,

I slept in a delightful oil hotel, jive; by the bridge which leads to Eton, the Thames still flowing under it as in old Spenser'e time ; the white swans sailing tranquilly upon the bosom of the tranquil stream ; the great round tower, and all the stately outlines of the castle just beyond. At ten o'clock the following morning I found myself once more before the Castle gates, intent on getting to the special service in St George's Chapel. It does not start until eleven, but already a great crowd besieged the massive archway. However, fortune favoured me again, and I was p-ivileged to have a sest jest by the a'tar and directly opp-site the Royal Gallery, And one by one the great folks troopel into the choir ; the leading guests ond the ,distinguieheel gentry of the neiehbourhnod ; the members of the Houeehould ; the Maids of Honour and the Royal Family itself. Some sat in the choir stalls, and some on chairs between these and the altar—the ladies all in deepest mourning and most closely veiled—whilst in the Royal Gallery— half hidden by the traceried stone—there sat the King, the Qeeen, and the Kaiser. A. more reverent assemblage or a stet Her service I have never seen. All hearts were thinking of the gracious figure lying there so quietly a few short steps away. The white faces, the black robes of the ladies, broken here and there by some bright military uniform, and vivigly nontrasting ' with the gorgeous banners of the Knights of the Garter hanging underneath the richle decorated windows, aed above the oaken carvings of the choir, the simple beauty of the altar, with its sweet white flowers ; the silvery-locked old Bishop of Oxford, seated just beside it, the strong and ruddy features of the Bishop of Winchesteewho gat with- in the altar rail beside him, the thrilling music of the organ, the deathlike etillnesa of the buihed and silent throng who rose from time to time as some ene of the royal bloo I came gravely in, the beauty of the psalms. the lessons, and the anthem, the thanksgiving for the late Sovereign, the prayers for the now King, who sat half hidden in the gorgeous gallery, the singing of the lovely hymn : "on the resurrection morning Soul and bo iy meet again, No more sorrow, no more sighing No more pain and then the silver-haired old I3ish op, rising from his seat and standing on the altar steps to pay his touching tribute to the dead Queen's memory, and make his brief, but beautiful, al- lusion to the King, and afterwards, once more the rich melodious wailing of that haunting Ftineral March, and then a vision of retreating figures in the gallery, the Week proces- eion passing from the chapel to the cloisters, the young Prince Edward following with his golden hair. It was a picture I shall not forget I I lingered ita the chapel precincts, looking at the wreaths. All round the choir, and far away behind the altar,and encompassing the proud old walls outside, and covering the great, broad western steps, they lay in such magnificent profusion as mere words are quite inadequate to paint. From kings and queens, and emperors, from princes, presidents, and peers; from gallant soldiers, learned scholars, lovely women,and tender-hearted little children in all ranks and walks of life; from states, and commonwealths,and celonie3 ; from venerable cities, toiling town', tiny villages, the fragrant tributes came ; and here and there amid the blaze of orchids and of arums, of roseseillies,scented violet s,of crowns,and shields,and harps, and towering pillars, and a hundred -exquis- ite devices, in a score of lovely hues, wee simple wreaths from Highland cottages away in the blue north, wet with the dew of loyal tears from honest hearts, hright with the rainbow hues of love's owa sunshine! I was turning thoughtfully away when I chanced to hear that all this wealth of flowers were not to be compared with those unvisited, and that the cloisters were "a perfect sea ' of bloom." But entrance was forbidden,[ found the Royal family had lately passed that way, and might, indeed, be lingering and, had it not been for the kindly offices of a policeman I had chanced to meet the previous night, I should infallibly have missed the crowning sight of all my journey. I shall never forget the scene. The clustered stone- work of the colonnade was almost hidden with such gigantic and resplendent wreaths as I had never even pictured in my wildest dreams, and the green lawn from end to end was absolutely buried in them. A huge pavilion had been erected to pro- tect them from the weather, but the sky peeped in beneath the canvas tenderly, and it is safe to say that never had it gazed upon a scene more fair. The richest'and the loveliest flowers the bosom of the earth has borne bad all woven into every grace- ful form which love could frame or fancy Could suggest; and she who, more than any other mon- arch in the world had worn" the white flewer of a blameless life "had won the whitest garlands of the whole world's love and gratitude, a t round the place where she was resting all teal's sweetest blooms were sending up their incense to the place where her'sweet soul was truly crewned. And so, high thoughts and dee,hl- 19 memories within my heart, I left the goad Queen sleeping, and came back to the aull world again.

NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that by an Order made by the Secretary of State. pursuant to the above it is provided inter alia as follows, viz. The time during which the killing and taking of r7;11:1 birds or an of them, i. prohibited by the Wild 11,Irds Protection y ction 'Act, 1880 (43 and 44 Vic„ cap 35), auali be extsryled in the County of Chester so as to be from the Ist day of March Zo the 12th day of August in each year. 7 The Act of 1880. as extended by Section 2 of this Order, shall anoly within the whole of the County of Cheater to the Kestrel and Heron as if those species were included in the Schedule of that Act. 4. The taking or killing of the following species of birds, namely :—King Fisher, Great Crested Grebe, Heron, Goldfinch, and Owl, shall be prohibited within the whole of the County a Chester during the whole of that period of the year to which the protection of wild birds under the Act of 1880 (as extended by Section 2 of this Order) does not extend. , 5. The taking or destroying of the eggs of the Owl, the Bittern, the King Fisher, the Kestrel, the Heron, the Sheldrake, the Dunlin, the Blackheaded Gull, the Corn- Tern, and the Oyster Catcher, shall be prohibited in the whole County of Chester 6. The Kidneys

The function of the Kidneys is to filter the blood. Disease is caused by the impurities left istile blood owing to the Kidneys not d g their work pro- perly. If the Kidneys are healthy the blood will contain nothing for disease to feed on. COUNTY BOROUGH OF W A REUNG CON. WAR, IN SOUTH AFRICA. The TRANSVAAL CAMPAIGN RELIEF COMMITTEE are reluctantly compelled to make a FURTHER APPEAL TO THE PUBLIC FOR FUND to enable them to continuo to support the Wives and Children of Reservists and Volun- teers now serving in South Alec.. The money in band will enable the Committee to continue this assistance for about six weeks longer and as a further Contingent of Volunteers has been ordered t , the Front the need of additional help is most urgent. -to The Committee therefore confidently appeal to the public fir renewed kelp in providing for the wives and dependents of the men who still endure, or are about to undertake the hvdships of the South African Campaign. Subscriptions may be.paid into any of the Banks or direct to the Hon, Treasurer, Bank House. HENRY ROBERTS. Mayor. Town Hall, Warrington, February 8, 1901,
